#6955. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

#6952. Artist Quentin Jones Launches a Surrealist Jewelry Collection

With Nocturne, the House of Quentin Jones founder brings the playful, sculptural language of her homewares to a collection of wearable art.

Above: Artist Quentin Jones wears pieces from her new jewelry collection while posing in front of tapestries Circe’s Garden and Western Moon.

British mixed-media artist Quentin Jones has quite the varied resume—one that spans artistic disciplines and collaborators as varied as Chanel, Apple, and Miley Cyrus. Now based in Brooklyn, her practice blends filmmaking, photography, collage, and painting.

Last year, Jones turned her eye towards interiors, launching a homeware line and design studio called House of Quentin Jones. The brand debuted with a collection of tapestries and ceramic sculptures that are both architectural and playful, leaning into Jones’s moody, dreamy look. Now, after a year of HOQJ, Jones is releasing new styles—and adding jewelry to the mix.

Nocturne, a collection of sculptural jewelry, translates Jones’s eclectic design language into wearable art. She pulled motifs like eyes, hands, and celestial forms from her tapestries and ceramics and incorporated them into the pieces. The collection features earrings, pendants, chains, and cuffs that balance black and white, pearl and onyx, organic and sculptural shapes.

“I’m interested in jewelry as sculpture,” Jones tells ELLE Decor. “A cuff wraps around an arm, a collar frames the neck, a tassel moves. The body becomes part of the composition. I want the pieces to have a presence of their own, while leaving room for the personality of the person wearing them.”

As she enters this new category, Jones is also beefing up her existing offerings. She’s adding new quilted tapestries to the studio’s repertoire and iterating on previous designs. With these new pieces, she says, “The palette has become softer and earthier—ivory, sand, and burgundy—but the compositions still have that surreal, graphic quality that runs through my work.”

But she isn’t stopping there. Jones has big dreams for the future of her eponymous studio, a “House” built larger with each new release. Next up, she’d like to expand the accessories category further, hoping to add bags, scarves, and other wearable pieces to her lineup.

“I’ve spent years working between art, film, and fashion,” she says. “I’m building towards a collection of things that can accompany you through your life—from what hangs on your wall to what you choose to wear.”

#6950. You Can See Everything You Ever Want to See in a Movie in You Can See Everything

Nathan Fielder’s new Elizabeth Holmes documentary is three hours long. You’ll wish it went on for three more.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

NATHAN FIELDER HAS become one of the greatest arbiters of what is—or isn’t—the truth. Fielder, whose mainstream career unofficially kicked off with his show Nathan for You, a dry, quasi-documentary, quasi-scripted, quasi-spoof of shows like Undercover Boss, has found a way to level up with each project he’s made over the course of his still relatively short career, each step of the way inviting audiences to not only question the reality of what he’s bringing to the screen, but their own stances and ways of thinking about things broadly.

The road for Fielder has included HBO’s The Rehearsal (another often surreal, often Lynchian project that presents as real, but on a subtextual level asks viewers to question whether any of what he’s depicting was really happening as it was shown) and The Curse, a Showtime/Paramount+ series that he starred in and co-created alongside Emma Stone and Benny Safdie. That show was, surprise, surprise, incredibly thoughtful, layered, and, ultimately, surreal when it comes to its underlying themes of sincerity and intention.

In a way, all of this has led Fielder to his latest project, the new A24 documentary You Can See Everything. The subject is a fitting one for Fielder: Elizabeth Holmes, the former Theranos CEO and convicted fraudster who was the subject of books (Bad Blood) and television shows (The Dropout) about her misdeeds. It makes total sense that someone famous for skirting the truth would be of interest for Fielder, a creator who has now spent so much time examining not only the truth, but what audiences might accept as the truth even given some reason not to.

In 2022, Holmes was sentenced to 11 years and 3 months in federal prison. That’s where You Can See Everything comes in: Holmes and her partner Billy Evans invited Fielder (and a film crew, led by co-director Lance Oppenheim) to join them in a home they were renting for complete access during the 34 days before she reported to federal prison in Bryan, Texas, in May 2023. And the film begins there.

And, quite frankly, if you’re considering seeing You Can See Everything, it’s best to leave it there. Fielder gets the access he was promised in the movie, but the joy of the film isn’t only in this exclusive vision of such a notable figure, but how an artist with such a relationship to the truth engages with someone who so notably has, seemingly, bent it. We also get to see Holmes and Evans shot as human beings, removed from any of the prior media circuses that have surrounded them. Fielder and Oppenheim put a fair camera to everything and let the camera do what was promised: See everything.

You Can See Everything has been one of the best-reviewed films of the year, and it lives up to the billing. Just when you think you understand what’s happening and what’s going to happen, you’ll be quickly proven wrong. Just when you think you have a handle on who someone is and what they’re all about… you’ll quickly be proven wrong. The movie is three hours long, which, of course, we know, seems daunting. But by the time it ends, you’ll wish it was going on for three hours more.

Below, we answer some of your questions about You Can See Everything—without spoiling any of the movie’s major twists or turns.

You Can See Everything is now available to watch at home. You can purchase it on Apple TV and rent it on Prime Video—and we suggest doing whichever of those you prefer.

You Can See Everything is, in essence, two movies in one. The first half of the movie is what the trailer and promotional materials for the movie billed: Nathan Fielder joins Elizabeth Holmes (and her new husband Billy Evans) in a home they rented for the 34 days before she reported to federal prison in 2023. This proves to be just as compelling as you’d imagine, as Holmes and Evans are both completely idiosyncratic characters. Moments like when Fielder shows Holmes Abbott and Costello’s “Who’s on First” bit are instant classics.

Eventually Holmes reports to prison in Bryan, Texas, and the movie is not even halfway over. It’s at this point that your eyebrow should raise, and you do the ‘leaning forward in seat’ meme—because this is when You Can See Everything gets truly wild. The timeline jumps forward two hours, and Nathan Fielder gets up to some of his mind-bending, Rehearsal-type business. At this point, we see some people’s true colors, and also meet some other unexpected guests and surprises in the movie. This second part of the movie is what will blow your mind, and leave you thinking long after the credits begin to roll.

You Can See Everything should be an absolute lock for a number of Oscar nominations, including Best Picture. And it should be a strong contender in one of the acting categories for one person whose appearance we will not be spoiling in this story.

Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim are the co-directors of You Can See Everything, and both have been key figures in American film and television culture for the last decade.

Fielder is a Canadian-born actor and director (and childhood friend of Seth Rogen) who rose to fame as the creator and star of Comedy Central’s Nathan for You. He’s dabbled in acting here and there—including a role as a limo driver in Rogen’s underrated holiday comedy The Night Before and in the sensational limited series The Curse—but his real calling has come in mind-bending documentary/comedy series like The Rehearsal. In season 2 of The Rehearsal, for example, Fielder actually got his pilot’s license while exploring the aviation industry. (And that was filmed between part 1 and part 2 of You Can See Everything, which is just bananas.)

Oppenheim, who is only 30, has also established himself as one of the best documentarians in the business. His movies Some Kind of Heaven and Spermworld along with his HBO series Ren Faire are deeply layered portrayals of very real people in very real situations; he shoots these documentaries in styles completely different from anyone else, though, lighting them like cinematic spectacles rather than what you probably think of as a documentary in your mind. (Some of the cinematography in You Can See Everything is unbelievably beautiful.)

Primetime, A24’s film about Chris Hansen and To Catch a Predator that came out last month and starred Robert Pattinson, Skyler Gisondo, and Merritt Wever, was Oppenheim’s scripted feature debut.

It probably helps to have information on Elizabeth Holmes’s case—which we recommend getting from any number of sources: The Dropout was the name of the Hulu series that starred Amanda Seyfried as Holmes, but it was also a well-researched and reported podcast that served as the show’s basis. There’s also an HBO documentary called The Inventor and a book called Bad Blood. Any of this would make for a good source for the crimes Holmes was accused of committing. The movie also includes a very brief recap of her case in the early going—not exhaustive, but enough to get the idea.

It does also make sense to have an idea of who Fielder is, what he does, and how he engages with his subjects. We recommend HBO’s The Rehearsal for that, but it’s not 100 percent necessary to enjoy and appreciate the film.

That’s the question, isn’t it? You Can See Everything explores the idea of truth, lies, perspective, and even documentary filmmaking itself. If it makes the most sense to you to take all of You Can See Everything at face value, then please go for it. If you feel the need to question everything, well, that’s okay too. That’s what makes Fielder’s work so unique and so immersive—you can think about it about 100 different ways, and no understanding or interpretation is wrong. It’s set up to feel like a kaleidoscope. You can understand something one way, but maybe something pops up, and suddenly you can’t think about it that way anymore. That’s the point—and that’s what you’ll think about long after the credits roll.

#6947. These Are the Home Trends Gaining Ground for 2027, According to Zillow

A new analysis of millions of for-sale listings reveals the features sellers are increasingly highlighting.

Above: A color-drenched den on Long Island, New York, designed by General Assembly.

Homes are getting smarter, but sellers are also advertising places to unplug. Zillow’s 2027 Home Trends Report finds growing mentions of both AI and tech-free spaces, along with color-drenched rooms, home offices, and “catios.”

The report tracks year-over-year changes in mentions of hundreds of features across millions of for-sale listings. The results reflect how sellers and agents are marketing homes—not a survey of buyers or evidence that a particular feature will help a property sell. Still, they offer a look at the amenities and design details increasingly making their way into listing descriptions. Here are the main takeaways.

Mentions of AI are up 119 percent, the largest increase in this year’s report. Zillow points to AI-powered appliances among the features sellers are advertising, while mentions of app-controlled home systems rose 34 percent.

Spaces to unplug are also appearing 34 percent more often. The report describes an interest in both technology that simplifies daily tasks and designated places to take a break from it.

“Digital detox is the new luxury,” says Amanda Pendleton, Zillow’s home trends expert, in a statement. “Homes are getting smarter, but buyers also want tech-free zones where nothing needs charging. Sellers and their real estate agents are now highlighting spaces to unplug.”

Color-drenched spaces—rooms where the same color extends across the walls, ceiling, and trim—are up 84 percent in listing mentions, making them one of the report’s biggest increases. Zillow sees the approach as part of a move toward bolder, more polished interiors. Exterior finishes are gaining ground, too: painted brick is up 27 percent, and board-and-batten siding rose 24 percent.

Color drenching takes a shade beyond the walls and onto the ceiling, trim, and other architectural details. The approach has even spawned a bathroom variation: tile drenching, which extends the same tile across surfaces for a continuous look.

The language used to describe these homes is changing alongside the features. Mentions of “elevated” rose 71 percent, while “cozy” fell 22 percent, “eclectic” dropped 19 percent, and “boho” declined 13 percent. Cozy remains one of the most common words in listings, but it’s appearing less frequently than it did a year ago.

Kitchen language is moving in the other direction. Mentions of “chef’s kitchens” fell 21 percent, and “gourmet kitchens” declined 15 percent.

Mentions of multigenerational living jumped 58 percent. Zillow connects the increase to homes accommodating the “sandwich generation,” or people caring for children and older family members.

Features that can serve different ages and needs are also appearing more frequently. Grab bars are up 18 percent, wider doorways are up 13 percent, and nurseries rose 9 percent. The report places these changes alongside the growing emphasis on flexible rooms and spaces with more than one use.

The home spa is changing. Mentions of spa-inspired bathrooms rose 60 percent, and cold plunge pools increased 44 percent. Jacuzzis and jetted tubs, meanwhile, each fell about 9 percent.

Spaces for exercise are also gaining ground. Home gyms are up 18 percent, while golf simulators rose 29 percent. Together, the findings cover both workout spaces and amenities marketed around rest and recovery, rather than a single version of the home wellness room.

Cats get a category of their own this year. “Catios”—screened-in outdoor spaces that let indoor cats spend time outside—are appearing in 32 percent more listings. A place to get some air, watch the birds, and keep an eye on the neighbors—the cat has a wish list, too.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6946. Winter Essentials Worth Giving—and Keeping

Bring warmth and classic style to city days and outdoor adventures with gear from The North Face.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

IT’s AN OFT-EXPRESSED sentiment, but it’s true: The holidays turn people’s attention toward friends, family, and community. There’s a beauty in the selfless, caring gifts people give to others. And while all that giving feels great, the holidays are also a good time to treat yourself. Plus, it’s getting colder.

Whether you equate The North Face with everyday city wear or outdoor adventures, it’s a solid, time-tested spot to get a gift for a loved one (or yourself). The brand has jackets and accessories with familiar winter looks, as well as products with less expected silhouettes that still offer protection from the elements in an understated style.

Here’s a look at some of the best cold-weather gear that will have you wrapping up the gift of warmth and durability… even if that gift is for yourself.

This is an easy gifting win because the warm, hooded, insulated jacket is a recognizable The North Face staple, evoking an unpretentious spin on the brand’s iconic Nuptse Jacket.

It’s familiar, but it’s also practical—the kind of gift you feel good about because it’s going to get a ton of use. (And it can withstand the repeated wears.) The attached hood offers protection from biting winds, and the straightforward color options make it easy to pair with a variety of outfits, whether it’s something fun or part of a heavy bundling during the coldest days of winter. This is the perfect type of winter coat: warm but not too puffy.

The Zaneck Jacket is a great alternative to the Aconcagua, stepping away from the outdoorsy look the brand is best known for. It’s still waterproof and insulated, but it has a longer, more modern cut.

The Zaneck comes with a removable faux fur lining for the hood, which, in addition to being warm, means there are a couple of different looks that can be teased out of it. The straight cuffs add to its stylish feel in a way that doesn’t scream “I’m dressed for warmth and not fashion,” even though it’s doing both.

The Lone Peak Triclimate 2 has a lot in common with the Zaneck. From a distance, they cut a similar silhouette. However, the Lone Peak adds a little versatility if you’re looking for a jacket that can handle a variety of climates.

A removable, insulated inner jacket is attached to a removable, insulated top layer, which is fit for rainy conditions or as a spring or fall jacket. The insulated layer converts the whole thing into a winter-ready jacket. It also sports underarm vents capable of releasing heat in the middle of a long hike, ideal for outdoorsy types. The hood is removable and it’s got adjustable cuffs and an adjustable hem, making this a do-it-all jacket.

It might be easy to ignore the Pertex Jacket. It’s a bit unassuming. It doesn’t cut as stylish of a figure as the Zaneck, but it’s endlessly useful. The lightweight Pertex fabric will keep you dry in a downpour, but it also offers warmth without marshmallow-like bulk.

The lightweight aspect makes this the kind of coat you toss in the backseat on the way to a hike, just in case. It’s also great for layering, whether it’s tossed on over a sweater or layered under an overcoat to make a favorite jacket more winter-ready.

This edition of the classic Base Camp Duffel echoes the design details we love in The North Face’s jackets. While it’s got enough style and a heap of color options for daily use, it’s built for adventure.

Made with water- and tear-resistant fabric, it boasts 71 liters of storage space, a roomier option to the similar 31-liter version. Like all of the brand’s products, it’s built as if the designers have been through the rigors of outdoor adventures (and they probably have). It’s got a sweeping D-zip opening, backpack straps, mesh pockets, and compression straps in case you’re curious if you can pack 73 liters worth of gear into the 71-liter space. (You know you’ve tried.)

The North Face has a lot of hats, none more iconic than this. The Salty Lined Beanie has a classic feel, fitting snuggly over the head with rib-knit texture, a cuffed shape, soft fabric, and ample color options to ensure it will match any jacket or accessory. The Salty Lined Beanie encapsulates the entire The North Face vibe in a small way: It’s stylish enough for a subway ride to work and practical enough for an outdoor adventure. Best of all, it’ll keep your ears warm in either instance.

Often, the best glove is the one that disappears into the outfit. It looks seamless, keeping hands warm without drawing too much attention. The Etip Recycled Glove is about functionality first and foremost, but the simple design and understated colors makes it look good with a variety of winter outfits.

While we all like to pretend we aren’t attached to our phones, the Etip makes a great modern gift because its stretchy fleece delivers a snug fit and the palms have touchscreen compatibility. That means you won’t have to take off your gloves to respond to that important text while the snow is falling.

#6945. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

#6944. 5 Fall Outfits I'm Stealing Straight From Taylor Swift

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Let's start with Swift's preferred autumnal color palette. She gravitates toward the season's rich jewel tones. Her style, which is often preppy or classic year-round with a vintage twist, includes paperboy hats, knee-high socks, colored tights, and plaid skirts. She's also embraced several of this season's trends, wearing wine-red hues, leather bomber jackets, and collegiate styles. So, it's safe to say both her current styling and vintage looks are encouraging me to upgrade my cold-weather fashion game. And while I am a fashion writer, you don't need to be a walking style encyclopedia—or global pop star at that—to master Swift's cold-weather ensembles.

Unlike the singer's glamorous red-carpet looks (I'm still not over that glittering ice-blue Dior gown from the 6th Annual Academy Museum Gala), her autumnal fashion is refreshingly down-to-earth, including attainable brands like Reformation and Anthropologie. Her ability to dress up simple staples like a black turtleneck with a few seasonal statement pieces has inspired how I think about pairing pieces in my own closet. Because her outfits are so normal, they're perfect for everything from a holiday party to a cozy date night. Ahead, I've rounded up my favorite Taylor Swift fall outfits of all time. You might as well hit play on Red while you're at it.

I’m still not over this preppy schoolboy-inspired look from 2023, which centers around a polo shirtdress and knee-high maple-hued boots. If it’s too chilly out to show your legs, simply slip on a pair of thermal tights. Keep the rest of the look simple with dainty jewelry. It’s one of those outfits that look effortlessly pieced together, hitting that sweet spot between casual and elevated.

Cynthia Rowley Cynthia Rowley Rugby Mini Dress

Creating an outfit that’s both cozy and chic enough for a dressy dinner can be tough—but this look from Swift is an easy solution. She layered a plush, oversized teddy coat over a simple, fitted long-sleeve top. Sheer tights under a plaid mini skirt dress the look up, while striking suede boots add more soft, autumnal texture.

Aritzia Original Contour Astral Longsleeve

For an elevated date night, I'd reach for a lace-trim slip dress. Swift made it cold-weather-approved with an oversized leather bomber jacket, which is one of this season's outerwear trends. While the pop singer opted for sandals, you can simply swap them out for black stiletto booties.

Charles & Keith Ally Ruched Chain-Handle Bag

Bordeaux red has become one of my favorite colors to style this season, and thanks to a co-sign from Swift, now I have even more inspiration. Swift’s towering cranberry heels serve as the visual focal point of this look, with a colorblocked polo and mixed-media pleated skirt from Thom Browne turning the outfit into a preppy maximalist’s dream.

You can take a break from the rich jewel tones every once in a while. Swift’s cloud-white ribbed mini dress is understated and sophisticated, with patent-leather accessories adding a subtle edge. Tie a bow around your ponytail for a girlish twist.

MZ Wallace Mini Crosby Sienna Quilted Hobo Bag

#6938. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

#6935. Inside Danielle Lauder’s Barbie Dream Closet

Designed by AMMOR Architecture, the ultra-pink, mega dressing room is a cinematic fantasy come to life.

Cher Horowitz had a touch-screen conveyer belt in her color-coded closet. Carrie Bradshaw kept her Manolos in an unusually spacious high-fashion-filled walk-in. And Margot Robbie’s Barbie displayed her hot-pink wardrobe like doll’s packaging in her wall-free fantasy villa. Danielle Lauder, the great-granddaughter of Estée Lauder, created her own iteration of the dream closet—this time, in real life.

Her own inspiration? “I always thought of Belle in Beauty and the Beast in the library, just rolling around on her ladder,” she says. “I’m not much of a reader, but as a collector, I knew that’s what I wanted.”

Her wish list for the jewel-box space also included curving walls, glossy pink lacquer, illuminated marble, and a capsule-shaped vanity inspired by the makeup collection she designed for Estée Lauder in 2019. The beauty empire was founded by—and named after—her great-grandmother in 1946; and her father, William P. Lauder, serves as the chairman of the board. (Danielle now oversees her own production company, Act 4 Artists.)

To make her dream a reality, Lauder turned to architects Goil Amornvivat and Thomas Morbitzer, founders of New York City–based AMMOR Architecture. The duo had instant chemistry with Lauder, who was especially taken with the meticulous diorama they created for her.

“Tom and I both love Barbie,” says Amornvivat. “So we tweaked our design process to make an architectural model that was more like a dollhouse, where you could switch out surfaces.” Their efforts worked on Lauder, herself a Barbie fan, and the pair walked away with the commission to not just create the dressing room but to overhaul the entire industrial-style apartment in the West Village of Manhattan, which was then being used as an art studio.

“They both really understood and saw my vision,” says Lauder. “Plus, they were the most excited, which made me more excited. Goil is like the Energizer Bunny.”

The 250-square-foot dressing room is, Lauder says, “the biggest room in the apartment besides the living room.” It’s also where Lauder spends the most time. She enters just off the primary bedroom via an arched passageway finished in custom bronze plaster, emerging from the dark portal into a pink fantasia where her expansive fashion collection is arrayed like a personal boutique. “The idea was for it to be part display area but also a functional closet,” she says.

At the center is a custom pink vanity outfitted with fluted drawers, dual-sided mirrors, and a top made of richly veined Midnight Rose marble, sourced from New York Stone. Lauder keeps her collection of vintage and reissued Estée Lauder compacts in the drawers. “I love collecting them and having them out on display or in a nice drawer,” says Lauder. “They’re beautiful little artifacts.”

On the curving walls, rounded niches display shelving and racks against illuminated stone, part of Lauder’s request for layered lighting. “I love the backlit marble,” she says. “It’s so flattering.” She keeps her enviable bag and shoe collection on permanent display, maintained with help from her housekeeper. Lauder draws from the selection on a daily basis, with particular affection for family heirlooms such as her mom’s vintage Dolce & Gabbana handbags.

Overhead, Amornvivat and Morbitzer devised a futuristic lighting installation, stretching mesh taut over an oval-shaped light source to create a seamless surface. “The light in there is controllable by color, and it's dimmable,” notes Morbitzer. “It’s kind of like a skylight, which was important because it’s the only room without windows,” adds Amornvivat.

The bespoke elements continue: heated floors, an ice maker, a perfume bar, plenty of drawers, and a pair of walk-in closets cleverly concealed behind matching lacquered doors. “The whole room is custom millwork,” says Amornvivat.

And Lauder did get the sliding ladder of her dreams. “That was my hill to die on,” she says. “I’ve always struggled to reach things in my closet, so it was definitely practical. And my grandfather had one in his apartment in the library when I was growing up, and I just loved it.”

Now that the closet is complete, Lauder has a hard time leaving. “I spend more time in here than I do probably even sleeping in bed,” she says with a laugh. The space is remarkably faithful to the original idea that Amornvivat and Morbitzer presented in their dollhouse diorama. “I wanted it to be totally mine, feminine and fun, and still very warm and inviting,” she says. “It’s become my ultimate bachelorette hub.”

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6931. Front Office: Radhi Devlukia

The host of the A Really Good Cry podcast turned to designer Jess Cooney to create a home office-meets-studio made for deep conversations.

Radhi Devlukia isn’t afraid of tears. On her podcast, A Really Good Cry, the whole point is to get personal, and to do so with guests as varied as supermodel Miranda Kerr, dermatologists, hospice nurses, personal finance advisors—and Devlukia’s husband, fellow podcaster Jay Shetty.

Now, two years into the show, Devlukia, a best-selling author, cook, and founder of beverage company Juni, is unveiling a new studio and office space located within her and Shetty’s Los Angeles house. Devlukia turned to designer Jess Cooney, who has a namesake Berkshires-based interiors firm, to craft a space designed to support these really good cries. Cooney had never tackled a podcast studio before but was ready for the challenge, which included building a room that could both serve as a set—technical components, artificial light, and all—as well as a welcoming home office.

From the start, Cooney knew what the space would not include. “We wanted to move away from the tropes usually seen in podcast studios, like styled bookshelves or cluttered backgrounds that detract from the people being interviewed, and also anything logo or merch heavy,” she says. “And toward something that felt warmer and more inviting.”

Instead, she took cues from spaces like fashion designer Bella Freud’s London apartment, Jean Cocteau’s Villa Santo Sospir in the Côte d’Azur, and Paloma Elsesser’s home by Gregory Rockwell. The resulting office, says Cooney, “has this incredible womb-like, feminine atmosphere that cocoons and holds you.”

Below, Devlukia takes our Front Office survey, giving a behind-the-scenes look at her new home base.

Radhi Devlukia: The hanging art piece in the middle of the window, Kissed by Katie Burdon.

When you need to lock in and power through work, what’s your setup?

Doors open, facing the window, instrumental music playing. I also move areas a lot during work hours to stay focused, so sometimes I’m at the desk, sometimes on the swivel chairs, sometimes on the floor by the window!

I have articles printed about my grandma and grandad on my canvas memory wall. They taught at schools in Africa in the 1970s and there were articles written about how much they were doing for the community. I also have vintage ceramics—I love the mix of old and new.

It really depends. If there are two of us, we’re on the swivel chairs. If there are a few of us, we’re around the marble table. We love keeping things casual at the office so sometimes people sit on the cozy rug or wherever feels comfortable.

What was the best meeting you’ve had there?

We have had so many creative meetings in this office. It’s such a calming and flowing environment. The whole rebrand of the podcast was ideated in this new office, which I’m obsessed with now.

What would you change about it if you could?

Maybe just having the ability to shoot with the blinds open.

What’s the funniest thing that happened there?

My team loves to prank me, so there’s been a lot of laughter in our office!

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

#6930. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

AI is moving beyond assisting cybercriminals and beginning to operate within live attacks, according to a recent report from Check Point Research. The technology is making sophisticated capabilities faster and more accessible while creating new security risks for businesses deploying their own AI systems.

#6924. All the Details on ‘Phony’ Season 2 for Everyone Obsessed

It’s been a moment since a show’s had me more locked in than Phony on Hulu, and with just one more episode left, inquiring minds (me) want to know: WHEN IS SEASON 2? And also important: is there even going to be a season 2?

Phony follows high schooler Sonny Cooke, who wakes up in the hospital after a car accident to find that his mom seems...off. For starters, her facial features are ever-so-slightly different—but there’s also the fact that she doesn’t remember their special way of saying “I love you” (three hand squeezes), and the fact that she’s just acting generally weird. Was she swapped out by an imposter? Does Sonny have Capgras syndrome? Is there some sort of alternate reality happening? Hopefully we’ll find out by the end of season 1, but then what? Which brings us to...

Not yet. But given how freely the cast and creators are talking about a second season (and the fact that the show is pretty popular), it seems likely to get a sophomore pickup.

Yes...but it’ll also end on a cliffhanger. Creator Nick Paley told Us Weekly “I’m really excited about the cathartic emotionality of the [season 1] cliffhanger. This thing kicks us into the next story in this world because I don’t like constantly depriving people of information. I want this to feel like it means a lot and it feels very important to the characters emotionally. So that cliffhanger does both. Every episode has very big turns at the end. When you’re telling a mystery, it’s really important to show it to people who don’t know.”

Paley told the Los Angeles Times that Phony Season 2 will feature “a whole new story” after the season 1 finale delivers “resolutions and answers.”

The majority of Phony Season 1 was filmed from March through May of 2026, and it premiered in October—a pretty short production time. So, if the show follows a similar schedule for season 2 and starts filming this spring, we could be looking at another October premiere.

Potentially a LOT. Connie Britton, who plays Ellen, told The Wrap, "I think Nick has this thing laid out through probably five or six seasons. He's so clear about what the story is, and so I would be very surprised if he has doesn't have Season 2 all ready to go."

#6923. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

AI is moving beyond assisting cybercriminals and beginning to operate within live attacks, according to a recent report from Check Point Research. The technology is making sophisticated capabilities faster and more accessible while creating new security risks for businesses deploying their own AI systems.

#6922. 10 Best Creatine Supplements of 2026, Tested by Us

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

Boost your workouts and build muscle with these dietitian-approved picks.

CREATINE SUPPLEMENTS CAN be an effective way to tap into more power during explosive exercise and build strength. Aside from protein powder, the best creatine supplements are the only supplements that can actually help you improve your fitness.

When you consume creatine (by way of supplements or real food), it turns into creatine phosphate in your muscles. This process helps create a key energy source called adenosine triphosphate, which is what your body uses for quick bursts of energy during intense workouts. In short, consuming creatine helps your muscles produce energy faster, and that extra energy lets you do more intense exercises, helping your muscles to grow bigger and stronger.

Sure, you could eat meat daily, but not everyone wants to devour massive portions of steak or salmon every day. (For reference, a two-pound steak has 5 grams of creatine.) That's where creatine supplements come in. Creatine is one of the most extensively researched supplements on the market, and its benefits are well-documented, but only a handful of creatine supplements are worth your money.

In collaboration with creatine expert Jose Antonio, PhD, C.S.C.S., a professor in the Department of Exercise and Sport Science at Nova Southeastern University in Fort Lauderdale, Fla., Men's Health fitness editors evaluated more than two dozen creatine products. Here's what we've found: Look for supplements certified by NSF or Informed Choice, and choose pure creatine monohydrate with five grams per serving—no fillers or extra ingredients.

To keep things simple, we calculated the price per standard five-gram serving for each supplement we ranked. Because, alongside quality, that's one factor you probably want to know.

Thorne Creatine provides 5 grams of easily mixable micronized creatine monohydrate and nothing else. It's NSF Certified for Sport and cost-effective at under 50 cents per serving, making it an excellent choice for nearly anyone looking to put on muscle. This is one of the brands most commonly used by athletes and is officially partnered with the UFC Performance Institute, being used by a lot of MMA fighters.

This powder has been my creatine supplement of choice for nearly two years, and I've been impressed with how it performs. It mixes easily, is truly tasteless, and doesn't have any of that gritty texture creatine powders can sometimes have. If you're new to performance supplements, Thorne's products are a reliable starting point, designed with a focus on minimal, clean, and transparent ingredients backed by clinical research for safety and efficacy.

Onnit's unflavored creatine provides 5 grams of micronized creatine monohydrate, straightforward, and sugar-free. No fancy flavors or frills here, just the good stuff. The small container allows you to try out this creatine without the obligation of buying a whole tub like other brands. This also makes it incredibly easy to fit into your gym bag, for those post-workout smoothies.

When mixing it into coffee, water, or juice, it never clumps. And it blends into a shake or smoothie perfectly, too. It's also Informed Sport certified, so you can trust it's been rigorously tested for banned substances and contaminants.

Transparent Labs' Creatine HMB offers a clean and effective 5-gram serving, devoid of any hidden ingredients. In my experience, it effortlessly dissolves, often requiring no additional mixing effort. Simply add a scoop to water or juice, and it seamlessly blends with the liquid, delivering a silky, trouble-free mix to enhance your workouts.For added nutritional benefits after a workout, I like to mix this creatine into my protein smoothies. Research suggests that a daily dosage of 3 to 5 grams of creatine monohydrate is effective for most individuals. Stick to this recommended dosage to maximize the benefits without overdoing it.

Look for a supplement that contains micronized creatine. This means the creatine particles are finely ground, making it easier for your body to absorb and utilize. This enhanced absorption can lead to better results and reduced stomach discomfort.

Creatine monohydrate should always be the key ingredient, but some supplements may include other additives. Opt for a single-ingredient, pure creatine whenever possible.

Choose a creatine supplement that undergoes third-party testing to ensure quality, purity, and safety. Look for certifications from reputable organizations such as NSF International or Informed Sport. These certifications provide an extra layer of confidence in the product's reliability and integrity. Remember, consistency is key when taking creatine. Stick with a creatine you don't hate so you'll actually take it. And of course, consult with a healthcare professional if you have any specific concerns or medical conditions.

As the Fitness and Reviews Editor at Men's Health, it's my job to be familiar with the best products in the world of wellness. For this story, I collaborated with Men's Health Deputy Editor Paul Kita, who heads our Food and Nutrition coverage. Our team also spent hours interviewing registered dietitians, medical doctors, and fitness professionals, as well as researching the top-rated and best-selling creatine supplements on the market.

The products in this guide undergo third-party testing, hold NSF, USP, or Informed Sport certification, and contain standardized forms of creatine in clinically studied, safe amounts. We focused on the most important features, including ingredients, dosage, price, and quality. The guide below features our carefully chosen picks for the best creatine supplements to buy right now.

Creatine is a naturally occurring molecule produced by our bodies and found in foods like meat, fish, and eggs. It's also available as a popular sports nutrition supplement, creatine monohydrate.

As a type of amino acid, creatine serves as a building block for proteins, which are essential for muscle, bone, and tissue development. Think of it as your muscles' secret fuel reserve, keeping them firing on all cylinders, particularly during intense activities.

Now, here's a cool fact: Despite its incredible performance-boosting benefits, creatine is not banned in any college or professional sports. That means you can confidently incorporate it into your training regimen without worrying about violating any rules or regulations, no matter who you are. (Although, as always, it's a good idea to consult your healthcare provider before adding new supplements into your diet.)

When you hit the gym and take creatine monohydrate, it supplies energy to your muscles, keeping them fueled for exercises. This leads to sustained power and endurance during workouts.By elevating your energy levels, creatine helps you push harder during strength training. It enables you to perform more reps, resulting in increased muscle stimulation and, consequently, more gains.Creatine is thoroughly studied, too. A 2022 analysis of 35 studies revealed that when combined with resistance training, creatine supplementation leads to a significant increase in lean body mass. Adults, regardless of age, gained over two pounds of lean body mass. Although it might seem small, it's a meaningful difference for those aiming to build lean muscle.

When stored in a cool and dry place, it can stay stable for years. Dry being the most important factor, as moisture can compromise and degrade the creatine crystals. But as long as it’s in a good environment, it’s a pretty sturdy supplement.

And even if it's expired, studies point to there being very little risk if you happen to take it. This means you are free to stock up if you come across a brand or deal that you’re happy with.

Dr. Antonio is clear: "There is absolutely no evidence that creatine supplementation causes baldness."So, what's with the rumors about creatine and side effects like hair loss? Concerns arose from a single study involving college rugby players who took high creatine doses. This study reported an increase in a testosterone metabolite linked to hair loss. However, it's vital to note that this study was short-term and on a small sample size, not replicated, and the observed variable in the creatine group remained within normal clinical limits.

Other research on creatine and testosterone levels found no significant changes. While the initial study raised questions, there's no solid proof linking creatine to hair loss. So, rest assured, creatine doesn't seem to be the culprit.

Throughout years of testing, we've found several excellent options. Yet, our standout choice for the best creatine supplement is Onnit Creatine Monohydrate. Onnit creatine holds the Informed Sport Certification, is uncomplicated, and free of sugar. With Onnit Creatine, you get 5 grams of micronized creatine monohydrate per serving, without any additives.

Years of using this supplement have confirmed that it easily mixes into coffee, water, smoothies, or juice without clumping, and it truly has no taste. At 50 cents per serving, Onnit falls in the middle of the pack in terms of price compared to other brands we tested, but you're paying for the quality and reliability of the ingredients. We also love the relatively compact packaging, which is practical enough to throw in a gym or work bag when needed on-the-go.

#6919. What Exactly Is an Aura Gap Relationship?

It’s all about matching and complementing energies, and it’s one of my non-negotiable.

When I first heard about the social media concepts of age, height, and swag gaps, I was confused as to how they could possibly threaten established relationships, or generally anyone who was into someone else. After all, these are generally known factors.

When you meet a new love interest, there will always be characteristics that reveal themselves over time—that's what dating is. Whether you meet someone in person or online, you generally know what you're getting into. Sure, you can lie on Hinge, but one IRL date should usually sets the facts straight.

Unless you're Pedro Pascal in Materialists or Demi Moore in The Substance, there's not much you can do about age and height gaps. Swag gaps, on the other hand, are a little more amenable. You can become a guy's stylist, educate him on the lingo, spots, and social niceties needed to play on the same aesthetically-pleasing field. It takes brute force, hard work, and someone willing to give you carte blanche with their time and dime, but it is possible to close that gap.

What is far more dangerous, explosive, and slow-killing is the aura gap. But what exactly is aura? It’s basically the vibe you give off, from your confidence, to your demeanor, to if you’re warm and personable or cold and short. It’s something you’re born with, not something you can buy. Sure, could you learn social skills and etiquette, of course. You could even pay someone to make you seem like you have good aura online, but the truth will eventually present itself in person. You can manufacture an image, but you can't concoct the energy you bring into a room.

When we consider aura-gap relationships and their longevity, I suspect the issue is less about understanding someone's faults and more fundamental to a partnership's long-term compatibility.

The best long-term example I can think of is my parents. My mom is larger-than-life, extroverted, crass, and will leave most people in ab-clenching laughter. My dad is brilliant, detail-oriented, and gets anything and everything done. While those attributes complement each other wonderfully, it's the indescribable warmth and ferocity the duo exude together that makes them such a good team. Their personalities are different, but their auras are entirely compatible.

And I think that's where the distinction lies. An aura gap isn't necessarily about one person being louder, funnier, or more charismatic than the other. It's about whether the energy you bring into a relationship is reciprocated, complemented, or quietly diminished.

Bad habits, political and religious differences, and financial compatibility are all big factors on marriage checklists, but aura, to me, is even bigger. You can agree on where to live, how many children to have, and what constitutes an appropriate amount of money to spend on dinner, but what good is all that if the person sitting across from you makes you feel like a less interesting version of yourself?

As we date, the question should be: does the person opposite me radiate a consistent energy that not only complements my own, but makes mine better? Aura is my non-negotiable, and frankly, it should be yours, too. I'd rather be with someone who makes me feel more myself than someone who makes me feel like I have to be enough for the both of us.

#6918. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

#6910. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

#6907. 11 Engagement Nail Ideas That Feel Romantic, Polished, and Photo-Ready

From milky whites to glitter tips, these manicures are made for ring photos.

Engagement season is officially upon us (I should know; I got engaged in December!), and with it comes a very particular kind of pressure: the perfect manicure for the perfect moment. Because once the ring is on, your hands become the main event, shared in photos, texted to loved ones, and passed around group chats.

But a great engagement manicure is about more than looking good on camera. At the end of the day, it comes down to preference and the way a set of nails makes you feel. Some brides-to-be find their calm in a sheer, milky pink; others feel most like themselves in a soft almond shape or a crisp, modern French tip. A glossy top coat might be your idea of perfection, while a hint of shimmer feels more you. There’s no wrong answer here. The best engagement nails are the ones that make you want to hold your hand out and admire it, because when you feel polished and put together, it shows in every photo, and in every happy-tear hug that follows.

Ahead, some ideas for the best engagement nails to wear while you celebrate, from the soft, understated shades that let your ring do the talking to the bolder finishes that show off your personality.

Polished and understated, this pearly white manicure is pure timeless elegance, with a subtle glow that sets the stage for the moment you say yes.

This toasted cappuccino mani brings warmth and richness to your engagement look, a creamy, neutral brown that flatters every skin tone and glows like the diamond against it.

A milky white manicure is anything but boring. It’s soft, sheer, and quietly luminous, with a clean, polished finish that makes your hands look effortlessly put together.

Delicate yet eye-catching, this manicure catches the light in all the right ways, making it a romantic choice that complements your ring without ever competing with it.

A sparkly manicure doubles as festive and romantic, making it an ideal pick for an engagement during the holiday season. With its glittering finish catching the light from every angle, it’s a celebration in itself.

Don’t be afraid to go with color! (I was wearing a dark espresso when I got engaged, and I wouldn’t change a thing.) A moody mauve is proof that a deeper shade can feel just as romantic as a soft neutral. Sophisticated and a little unexpected, it’s an excellent pick for anyone who wants their engagement manicure to feel unmistakably them.

A deep cocoa cat-eye with textured dewdrops gives depth and personality to your engagement look, with a rich chocolate base that shimmers and shifts as your hand catches the light. The glossy droplets add a playful, dimensional touch, perfect for anyone who wants something a little different that still feels romantic and perfectly suited to a winter celebration.

Nearly bare nails featuring a tiny French manicure are proof that less really is more. With a sheer, natural-looking base and a delicate whisper of white along the tip, this look feels fresh, minimal, and effortlessly refined.

Another shade that plays into the holiday season, this metallic silver manicure brings a cool, icy shimmer to your engagement look. Sleek and glamorous, it pairs beautifully with platinum and white gold bands and makes a diamond sparkle even brighter.

Subtle yet equally stunning, this glazed ombré blends a light beige base into soft brown tips for a look that feels warm, modern, and beautifully blended. It’s a lovely pick for anyone who wants a neutral manicure with a little extra depth, and it lets your ring shine while still feeling special every time you glance down.

So glam, so chic, this silvery white nail set brings a frosty, high-shine finish to your engagement look, with a metallic gleam. Polished and dazzling, it’s a standout choice for anyone who wants their manicure to feel luxe and celebratory.
