#6839. The 94 Best Movies of 2026 So Far (and 11 More We Can't Wait For)

With Christopher Nolan, star-studded epic romance, and major Marvel events, this list is a behemoth. And it also features a movie called Behomoth!.

AS A HUGE fan and follower of both movies and sports, the former has one major advantage: There's always something to look forward to. As a fan of the New York Jets, I don't always have that luxury. As a fan of watching films, though, not only is there always something worth my time on the big screen or streaming, but there's always several flicks down the line that will sound great or intriguing for one reason or another. Will they all turn out great? Of course not. But with sports and movies alike, excitement tends to be all about hope.

After a particularly strong 2025—movies like One Battle After Another and Sinners proved to be true standouts—how does 2026 pick up? With strength and energy, that's how. The year has been filled with all sorts of great movies, from horror (like 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple and Obsession) to epics (like The Odyssey), to some pretty great comedies (like Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie and The Invite). It’s been a good year.

But we always like to look at not only what’s been good to date, but also what we can still be excited for—and that includes everything. We’re talking stuff on the calendar already, stuff we suspect could come out this year, and even stuff making noise in places like the Sundance Film Festival. Throughout the year, this list evolves from our most anticpated movies into our favorite movies, and always looks quite different in December than it did in January.

For now, we've got a lot we’ve loved, but still a lot to look forward to as well. Below, we've listed nearly 100 movies on our radar that we think you'll be really interested in seeing.

If you're interested in a killer chimpanzee B-movie slasher, then Primate is for you. The movie delivers what it promises. Ben the chimpanzee does some very terrible things on screen but it makes for quite an enjoyable experience.

28 Years Later: The Bone Temple is a direct sequel to last year's 28 Years Later. And while the great Danny Boyle doesn't return to direct, the movie is helmed by the more-than-capable Nia DaCosta and once again counts Alex Garland as its screenwriter. The real assets, though, are the movie's leads: Ralph Fiennes and Jack O'Connell are sensational, magnetic every minute they're on screen amidst all sorts of post-apocalyptic chaos. Bring on part three.

Here's a quick pitch for The Rip: Matt Damon and Ben Affleck. The longtime friends team up once again for The Rip, a cop thriller in the vein of movies you've probably seen several times before... but done particularly well. This twisty crime story involves a police team finding a ton of money in a stash house (the titular rip), and figuring out who may or may not be corrupt. In addition to our beloved duo, the cast is shockingly stacked: Steven Yeun, Kyle Chandler, Scott Adkins, and One Battle After AnotherAcademy Award nominee Teyana Taylor are all on board.

SAM RAIMI FANS, ASSEMBLE! For the first time since 2009, the master of gross effects and frenetic cameras is back with an original horror story in Send Help. Rachel McAdams plays a Survivor superfan who gets stuck on an island with her dickhead boss (Dylan O'Brien), and then the antics begin. Think Cast Away meets Misery meets Drag Me to Hell. It's great stuff.

Jason Momoa and Dave Bautista are a pair of cool dudes who, with The Wrecking Crew, set out and did exactly what they intended to do: Make a badass buddy action comedy. The movie finds the pair playing estranged brothers who reunite in Hawaii to investigate their father's mysterious death; From there, we get lots of jokes, and a lot of (very violent!!!) action. It's a good time! Honestly, if they want to make a couple sequels to this, I'd be her for it.

Part This Is Spinal Tap, part Josie and the Pussycats, and part Curb Your Enthusiasm, The Moment is Charli XCX's movie for anyone who enjoys her music, persona, or both. A really fun movie about music, stardom, and celebrity in 2026.

Pillion was technically released initially in 2025 (and was also included on our list of favorite movies of that year), but we didn't want to miss an opportunity to recommend it to people once again. This unique film follows a man (Harry Melling) who engages in an unexpected BDSM relationship with a biker he meets at random (Alexander Skarsgård). The movie is quite the tonal balancing act, at times funny, intense, raunchy, and heartbreaking, but it all comes together by the end. A delightful movie, and a great showcase for both leads.

The classic novel comes to life on the big screen once again, this time from Promising Young Woman and Saltburn director Emerald Fennell, and with Margot Robbie and Jacob Elordi in the lead roles. Fennell, an Academy Award winner for her work on Promising Young Woman, has both big fans and big detractors at this point—but her take her, while book purists haven't been thrilled, is a big, visually stunning epic romance. Robbie and Elordi are both up to the task as well, bringing a charged energy to roles that really need it. Alison Oliver, who recently shined on HBO's Task, is another major highlight in a supporting role. An original soundtrack from Charli XCX helps to set the anachronistic mood and feels like a real cherry on top.

This action-thriller, based on a book by Don Winslow, looks like a very solid entry in the genre that we're going to call "Somewhat Resembles a Michael Mann Film." And reviews have confirmed as much—the movie has often been compared to Heat, and while its tough to compare to Mann's 1995 masterpiece, we'll take just about anything in that vein. Look: If someone tells you there's gonna be a crime thriller in the vein of Heat with Chris Hemsworth, Mark Ruffalo, Halle Berry, and Barry Keoghan leading the cast, how could you not be on board?

One of our coolest directors, Gore Verbinski, makes his grand return with Good Luck, Have Fun, Don't Die—his first movie since 2016's A Cure For Wellness. If you don't know Verbinski by name, you should know several of his movies: the original Pirates of the Caribbean trilogy, The Ring, and Rango are among the standouts. Good Luck follows Sam Rockwell as a man who claims to be from the future and fighting off a rogue artificial intelligence. While the movie takes some pretty tough and labored tangents into on-the-nose satire, Rockwell's performance and the practical effects keep the movie afloat as a fun sci-fi romp.

Despite the title, you don't need to know anything else about Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie before going to see it. Sure, this story of two inept dudes in a band who just want to play music at a bar—and go through all sorts of ordeals, including time-travel, to do it—has existed before in both web series and television series form. But you can just go in and laugh and the absolute madness of the movie. Trust us—you'll have a good time with this one if you're into "madness that makes you laugh." This is a long-term passion project of Matt Johnson, who became well known recently for directing the underrated BlackBerry. His brash talent is again on display here.

We're always in on Glen Powell, and How to Make a Killing marks his first foray into A24's world as a leading man. This black comedy neo-noir comes from Emily the Criminal director John Patton Ford and follows Powell as a man who decides to take out a few members of his family in order to increase his chance at making a whole bunch of money via inheritance. The supporting cast joining Powell isn't too shabby: Margaret Qualley, Jessica Henwick, Ed Harris, and Topher Grace are all along for the fun.

The Napa Boys is a very specific cut for comedy fans. Remember when all those nondescript American Pie direct-to-video sequels were coming out? "American Pie Presents:.."? The Napa Boys imagines itself as one of those: self-impressed raunchy comedy with little tact—crossed, naturally with the wine bro nature of 2004's Sideways. As a result, we get a movie that presents as a sequel to a franchise that both has long been running out of juice, and, in our world, never was. If you get the truly absurd brand of parody being presented here, you're going to get a real kick out of it.

Hoppers feels like a throwback to the golden age of Pixar. Not only is it an original story (at a time when the legendary animation studio has often been leaning into sequels, prequels, and more), but it's a sweet and funny one at that. Following an animal-lover who uses Avatar-style technology to transfer her consciousness into a robotic beaver body that can discover what animals are thinking and saying, Hoppers actually turns into something of a conspiracy mystery story. With a voice cast that includes Jon Hamm, Dave Franco, and Meryl Streep among others, it's a fun one for the whole family.

Look. This is one of those movies that you really don't have to overthink. It's Alan Ritchson—Reacher himself—taking on a giant alien robot monster thing. This is as much of a throwback to '80s-style action filmmaking as we're ever going to get, and it delivers as promised. The fact that the rest of the cast includes staples like Jai Courtney, Esai Morales, and Dennis Quaid is just a bonus.

Undertone has been positioning itself as A24's next huge horror hit—and we'll see how it lands. The filmfollows a supernatural podcaster who gets sent some particularly spooky recordings; Its soundscape has brought tons of hype, with many calling it the scariest experience of the year. A simple premise like that sometimes makes for a very solid horror movie.

Based on the novel by Andy Weir(who also wrote The Martian), Project Hail Mary is a story of space exploration and a regular guy (played by Ryan Gosling) who gets involved. Phil Lord and Chris Miller (who are responsible for the Spider-Verse movies and directed 21 Jump Street) are behind the camera for this one, which has already proven to be a big, big hit. Gosling is basically on his own out in space for most of this, but it also includes a fun little alien guy named Rocky and a nice supporting performance by Anatomy of a Fall star Sandra Hüller. The visuals here are worth seeing on the biggest screen you can find.

2018's Ready or Not was one of the most fun horror films of the last decade, and now eight years later we get to see Samara Weaving's lead sucked back into the same twisted, bloody, violent games. The supporting cast—including David Cronenberg (!!!), March MH Flex star Elijah Wood, Sarah Michelle Gellar, and The Pitt's Shawn Hatosy—is fantastic. The movie kind of keeps with the form of the first Ready or Not, so if you liked that one, you'll certainly have a fun time with this one.

Forbidden Fruits follows a secret coven of witches operating in a shopping mall. Like, guys, some premises you just have to accept for how good they are and see them out. The movie is a campy, fun, twisty turny ride. Understand what you're getting into—a little bit of horror, a little bit of satire, and some fun twists—and it's going to be a good time.

Zazie Beetz (Atlanta, Deadpool 2) gets to lead her own horror/thriller movie with They Will Kill You, where she plays a woman who answers a help-wanted ad for a housekeeper in a luxury apartment building, only to find herself fighting for her life. The movie is pretty similar in tone to fellow horror/comedy Ready or Not 2, which we just talked about. But They Will Kill You leans more into its John Wick-style action and Kill Bill style plot, which sets it apart. The supporting cast, which includes Patricia Arquette, Heather Graham, and Industry star Myha'la, make it extra worth it.

#6834. The 61 Best New Shows of 2026 (and 1 More We Can't Wait For)

We've got murder mysteries, stories of love and lust, and lots and lots of Yellowstone. And that's just the beginning.

THE IDEA OF "new" television shows is a little bit of a tricky one these days. Sure, we do still get a fair share of truly new, exciting, unique ideas each year—just look at shows like Severance a couple years ago and Pluribus last year. Shows like last year's The Lowdown—rich, well-written, character-focused stories—still draw major stars like Ethan Hawke and still hit in a big way for audiences that watch it. If someone was being dramatic and wanted to say original TV was dead, dying, or on life support, there's plenty of evidence out there to throw in their face.

But at the same time, they aren't entirely wrong. For one reason or another, so much of the TV landscape—even shows we're really excited for—are based around something that in one way or another already exists.

Look, we can't beat too much around the bush: People love nostalgia, people love things they recognize and remember, and it can be pretty hard to get anyone to even attempt to give their attention to something new, let alone get really into it in the way we all like to with the very best of TV. Which is why we kind of have two different options here—people need to be really trusting of word of mouth and really aware when something is a really good new, original show (because no one likes to waste their time with a piece of junk), and we just need to really hope that all the remakes, reboots, reimaginings, and re-whatevers are good.

And they should be! HBO's Lanterns is modeled after True Detective, stars Kyle Chandler and Aaron Pierre as the titular Green Lanterns, and counts Watchmen genius Damon Lindelof as a producer. Of course it’s good. Below, we're going to keep track all year. We're charting the shows we already know are good. We're charting the shows we think might be good. And we're charting the shows that may not even come out this year, but if they do... they still might not be good. But, again, say it with us: We're choosing to be hopeful.

Netflix has been really good of late at the pulpy, potboiler, limited series, and His & Hers is the latest in that winning streak. The show follows a detective (Jon Bernthal) and his estranged journalist wife (Tessa Thompson) as they both hunt for answers when a local woman—whom they both have a close connection to—winds up dead. Based on Alice Feeney's book of the same name, His & Hers is exactly how this kind of show should be: Juicy, fun, easy to watch, and filled with really good actors.

In shrinking its story down to a more intimate one that takes place between the margins of major universe events, Game of Thrones takes a page out of the Andor handbook with A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms (based on George R.R. Martin's Dunk and Egg novellas). This short series, which follows a big, humble, anxious hedge knight (former professional rugby playerPeter Claffey)and his little friend/squire (Dexter Sol Ansell) is a true breath of fresh air for Thrones fans on both a story and character level. It's a delight.

Doesn't the premise of Ponies just sound fun? This spy series set in '70s Moscow follows a pair of women working with the CIA who find themselves at the midst of a vast conspiracy when their husbands both die under mysterious circumstances. And those women are played by The White Lotus's Haley Lu Richardson and Game of Thrones queen Emilia Clarke. Sometimes something that sounds like a winner just turns out to be one.

The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins (NBC/Peacock)

This show about a disgraced former football player (Tracy Morgan) and a documentary filmmaker following him (Daniel Radcliffe) comes from the team behind 30 Rock, so you know you're going to get several jokes per minute. Plus, Morgan and Radcliffe are no slouches themselves. The show had a special debut in January and will continue on a regular schedule later in February.

At the very least, Wonder Man is among the best shows Marvel ever made—and you could make a case that it's at the top of the list. Yahya Abdul Mateen II plays the titular hero (aka actor Simon Williams) as the kind of guy we love to love in this sort of story: humble, well-meaning, charismatic, and ambitious. But when he meets fellow actor (and former fake terrorist) Trevor Slattery (Ben Kingsley) and learns that his favorite movie from childhood is being remade by an acclaimed director, everything changes. Wonder Man isn't just a good Marvel show, but a good show about people, movies, and friendship period. Let's hope the MCU takes notes from what worked so well here and keeps the streak going in the future.

One of America's great cultural institutions made its grand return in 2026: The Muppet Show! This version—coming from executive producers Seth Rogen and Evan Goldberg—is directly modeled on the original Muppet Show, which means special guests, musical performances, comedy sketches, and lots of fun. The first episode, which featured special guest star Sabrina Carpenter, was an absolute delight for anyone who likes Kermit, Miss Piggy, and friends (or who just likes laughing and having a good time). For now, it's just this one episode, but if the reaction is strong enough (and it seems like it might be), the show will come back in full.

The latest entry in producer Ryan Murphy's American Story universe is called Love Story, and follows the romance and life between Carolyn Bessette Kennedy and John F. Kennedy Jr. The show is, however, much better than that tabloid-y description sounds; Sarah Pidgeon, who plays Carolyn, is a star, coming off her Tony-nominated role in the Fleetwood Mac-inspired play Stereophonic. But Paul Anthony Kelly, who plays JFK Jr., is a real discovery—it's his first role (he was previously a model), and he truly feels like a reincarnation of the late attorney/magazine publisher/dashing public figure. Of course, this is a story that ends in an extreme tragedy—so if you get invested, be sure to have your tissues ready.

This wild documentary series looks at different disputes between neighbors, telling compelling and often escalating true stories that will truly floor you in the depiction of their very-real truth. Think about this less as a story about the legalities of these disputes, and more a look into some larger-than-life characters through an often trippy, absurd lens. The series is produced by Josh Safdie and Ronald Bronstein, coming off of their recent success on the Oscar-nominated Marty Supreme.

The new season of Scrubs brings back almost all of the original cast, including Zach Braff, Donald Faison, and Sarah Chalke. And audiences who love and/or remember that trio and most of the show's original dramedy premise should be pleased with this new version of the beloved aughts sitcom. The last season aired in 2010, so we're OK with calling this 16-years-later revival a 'new' show.

DTF St. Louis follows trio of languishing adults—played by the electric trio of Jason Bateman, Linda Cardellini, and David Harbour—who get involved in a love triangle and general madness that results in one of them winding up dead. You may think you have an idea of what you're getting into with this show based on the cast and that premise, but believe us—all of your expectations will be upended. DTF St. Louis is a tonal balancing act, a show that bounces between hilarious dark comedy, compelling mystery, and gutting, relatable drama. It's one of the best shows of the year.

The second age of Yellowstone begins in earnest with Marshals, a 13-episode drama that airs on CBS (and streams on Parmaount+ either simultaneously or a day later, depending on which tier of subscription you have). The series focuses on Luke Grimes's character Kayce, and how an old SEAL Team leader (Logan Marshall-Green) recruits him into his Marshal squad. There's at least one more Yellowstone spinoff coming in 2026—Dutton Ranch—and so this is the one leading the charge.

One of the busiest men in show business these days—on both the big and small screen—is Guy Ritchie, and his newest endeavor takes him back to a world he's inhabited a couple times before. Young Sherlock follows, well, a Young Sherlock Holmes. And as you can imagine, it follows Arthur Conan Doyle's famous detective as he solves a mystery. Hero Fiennes Tiffin plays the titular character, and Colin Firth is also part of the show's cast. If you're into that, you'll have a fun time! It's an enjoyable show.

The erotic thriller is back! Or, well, juicy erotic character study dramedy? Whatever we want to call it, Netflix's Vladimir is one of the year's most exciting new shows. Based on the novel of the same name, this show follows a longtime literature professor at a college (Rachel Weisz, constantly breaking the fourth wall) who finds herself in the midst of controversy when her fellow professor husband (John Slattery) gets caught sleeping with students. She takes this as an opportunity to switch things up a bit, and so she becomes smitten with her very sexy new colleague (Leo Woodall). It's a fun, zippy, juicy time—and kind of like if Deadpool was actually about a smart woman in academia who's spinning her wheels and maybe just a little bit horny.

From the mold of Ted Lasso and Shrinking comes Rooster, which stars Steve Carell and counts Lasso and Shrinking co-creator Bill Lawrence (who also made Scrubs and Cougar Town) as its showrunner. Carell plays an author who heads to help his professor daughter on a college campus... and becomes enamored with life there. Imagine Back to School but with some middle-age soul-searching and academia at play? Sure! HBO's got another winner on their hands with this one—and maybe Carell can get the Emmy that he somehow never got for The Office.

There are 29 books in the Scarpetta series, and, now, one TV show on Prime Video starring Nicole Kidman and Jamie Lee Curtis. The series follows forensic pathologist Dr. Kay Scarpetta (Kidman) as she solves a crime—but the show is so much more than just that. In a way, Scarpetta is destined to fit into Prime Video's roster of rock-solid procedural murder mysteries, which also includes Reacher, Cross, Bosch, and plenty more. But it's got a little bit of an extra chaotic oomph—helped out by the star-studded cast that also includes Bobby Cannavale and Simon Baker—that make it worth your while and a unique, fun show.

Sunny Nights, which aired in Australia last year, now makes its U.S. debut on Hulu. The premise is simple: A pair of siblings (played by the very funny Will Forte and D'Arcy Carden) want to start a spray tan business, but wind up entrenched in the criminal underworld. Whoops! Forte and Carden have been two of our finest comedic actors for a while—with ability to play depth in addition to that humor—and the show thrives when they're at their strongest. A solid watch for anyone looking for something light but compelling.

This series—from Yellowstone super writer/producer Taylor Sheridan—follows Stacy Clyburn (Michelle Pfeiffer) and her family, as they move from the busy, stressful world of New York City to a more rustic setting out in beautiful Montana after a totally unforseen tragedy. Kurt Russellplays her husband, Preston, with his usual endless charisma, while the supporting cast includes the likes of Matthew Fox and Suits star Patrick J. Adams. Sheridan does his usual zigging and zagging in The Madison—the tone can sometimes shift from very sad to zany and comedic, and, as usual, there's a bit of social commentary speechifying buried in almost every scene. But if you're on board with the writing style he uses in shows like Yellowstone and Landman, you'll probably be OK with it here, especially if you're ready for a heavier and more contemplative tone. This show was originally planned to be part of the Yellowstone connected universe, but those plans have since been changed; It's now a new story, but with that Montana setting, you still get to look at all sorts of beautiful landscapes.

After striking gold a couple years ago with Jury Duty, Prime Video gives its grand Truman Show-esque experiment another try with a second season called Company Retreat. This time around, we follow a regular guy who thinks he’s working as a temp for a hot sauce company and joining them on their annual company bonding retreat. Of course, everyone around him is actually an actor in a scripted, engineered situation. It’s a little goofier than the first season, but seeing how everything plays out is compelling, often very funny television nonetheless. A worthy follow-up, and, honestly, we might just watch these as long as they feel like making them.

Riz Ahmed is the creator and star of Bait, a show about an actor who thinks he's about to get offered the role of a lifetime—James Bond!—and the madness that spirals out of such a revelation. If you're a fan of Ahmed's (and why wouldn't you be?) this will be a fun ride.

Something Very Bad Is Going To Happen (Netflix)

Camila Morrone (Daisy Jones and the Six) and Adam DiMarco(The White Lotus) are set to get married in this horror series from showrunner Haley Z. Boston (who worked on the very underrated Brand New Cherry Flavor) and producers The Duffer Brothers. And not to oversell, but Something Very Bad Is Going to Happen is the best horror show I've seen in quite some time. While the show plays with familiar settings and tropes—you may think of Ready or Not or Midsommar during the show's eight episodes—it's so filled with Ari Aster-esque dread and stunning, striking camera work that it doesn't even matter. Horror fans are in for an absolute treat with this one.

#6832. These Are the 46 Best Action Movies Ever Made

AS LONG AS there are movies, there will be action movies. Why? Because no young girl or boy grows up pretending to be a damsel in distress or a sidekick. They all want to punch, draw swords, and run toward the danger. They are the heroes and the future directors, choreographers, and performers who bring these imaginations to life.

And to the incredulity of your father (who insists no movie will ever top First Blood) the action genre hasn’t slipped on it’s roundhouse kick. If anything, we are now experiencing another golden age of ass-kicking cinema. While there are obvious developments in VFX technology, which have allowed for larger set pieces and more realistic thrills, much of what makes a great acton film a great action film hasn’t changed: painstaking choreography, fearless stunt performers/actors, and clear cinematography (and not that Taken fence jumping bullshit.)

The only thing that’s changed are the names and faces. Legend action directors like Ang Lee, John Woo, Robert Clouse, and Zhang Yimou (still going strong) have given way to newer arrivals like Gareth Evans, Chad Stahelski, and Christopher Nolan. And performers like Bruce Lee, Jean-Claude Van Damme, and Arnold have changed to Tony Jaa, Iko Uwais, and Charlize Theron.

If anything, the fights are becoming more brutal, more carefully choreographed, and more ambitious. And international actors and directors are finally getting increased attention (and direction) in a Hollywood overly-saturated with slapstick cinematography and post-production punches.

Here’s hoping for more John Wicks, more Raids, and more Atomic Blondes in the future. Until then, here is, in no particular order, the 36 best action movies ever made.

John Rambo would become increasingly cartoonish as the sequels went on, but in the original, he was just a scared veteran trying to survive out in the real world.

The Raid was more frenzied, but the sequel (also written and directed by Gareth Evans) got grand, introducing an intricate crime-drama plot. Didn’t matter: Redemption is brilliantly violent, a ballet of broken bones, perfect punches and crazy car chases.

The film marked the breakout moment for Muy Thai martial artist Tony Jaa and the beginning of one of the best action franchises ever. There’s not a lot of fakery in Jaa’s fight scenes. Dudes are getting punched for real.

The second film is more dialogue-heavy, but the first half is essentially nonstop action … and it’s a good thing the Crazy 88 fight is in black and white, because it’d almost be too bloody to watch otherwise.

What should have been Jason Bourne’s final adventure is a thrilling, satisfying conclusion to this kinetic trilogy. Director Paul Greengrass and star Matt Damon permanently reshaped how we think of the globetrotting espionage thriller. James Bond is still playing catch-up.

Mission: Impossible – Rogue Nation (2015)

You can’t go wrong with any in this series – though it has only gotten better as the years have gone along – but the introduction of Rebecca Ferguson to this series just amped up the emotional stakes.

Keanu Reeves’ first of two masterful franchises on this list, John Wick gave us a mournful 21st-century assassin who won’t let his grief get in the way of shooting every bad guy in the head at point-blank range.

Bruce Lee was popular before this film, but this is what sent him into the stratosphere. Well-made from start to finish, and absolutely unrelenting. The best part about it is that there isn’t a gun to be found in the whole film.

Ong-Bak gave us Tony Jaa and Muy Thai, and Ip Man gave us Donnie Yen and Wing Chun. While Yen had been in many other great action films previously (check out Kill Zone — S.P.L.), its the Ip Man films that will define his legacy, the character forever his—the semi-biographical person of Ip Man, Bruce Lee’s trainer. Though the films can feel a bit jingoistic at times (they’re basically Hong Kong’s Rambo), the fight scenes are femur-breaking legendary.

Zhang Yimou’s magnum opus, Daggers is maybe more love story than a martial arts film—and so the most action-packed love story of all time—but “love story” is no cinematic pejorative. The film features the most colorful and vibrant cinematography of any film on this list. Action is a visual genre. And no film here is more pleasing to the eyes.

John Woo’s Hollywood career wasn’t as sparkling or consistent as fans would have hoped, but this late-‘80s peak remains a glorious salute to melodramatic plotting and poetic shootouts. Chow Yun-fat made for a fierce, soulful assassin — and, never forget, The Killer is where Woo began his obsession with dove-filled action scenes, which have been parodied everywhere from 21 Jump Street to Scary Movie 2.

Oh, don’t dare not think of this as an action movie. The existential dread of Ridley Scott’s original is replaced by James Cameron’s trademark intensity, and even though this has aliens and spaceship, this is as concentrated a dose of thriller mania as you will find. You’re gripping your chair just thinking about it, aren’t you?

George Miller had been mulling a new Mad Max film for years, and clearly, he spent that time coming up with every possible permutation of a chase sequence. This movie remains thrilling, almost insane, four years later.

Because of Ang Lee’s prestige and the glamorous international cast, there is sometimes a sense that this film is bland, good-for-you Important Filmmaking. But watch it again today. This movie is iconic for a reason: it's a blast, and a euphoric ride.

If the first installment was more of a gritty thriller, then T2 is where director James Cameron cranked up the action spectacle. (That’s the kind of thing that happens when you have a much bigger budget.) Harnessing the power of then-new CG technology, this sequel threw down a challenge at the start of the ‘90s that other blockbuster filmmakers struggled to answer.

Men in Black is that perfect mix of boot-chasing, gun-firing acton with so much outer-wordly originality, Hollywood is still trying to suck every last ounce of life-force out of the 1997 original. Can we just leave the classics alone?

Yes, the car chases are as amazing as you’ve heard, but what’s striking today is how well this holds up as a cop procedural. Steve McQueen was an incredible movie star, and he was never better than he was here.

Popeye Doyle is the perfect action star for the ‘70s: grizzled, cranky, morally compromised, not the least bit likable, but absolutely tenacious about getting the bad guy, even if it’s for reasons he can’t even necessarily comprehend. Gene Hackman was at the absolute peak of his powers at this point.

Hollywood remade Akira Kurosawa’s masterpiece as The Magnificent Seven. It couldn’t compare: Not only is Seven Samurai an epic of scope and scale, it’s one of the most emotionally nuanced of action movies, featuring a slew of characters you come to care deeply about.

Michael Mann’s epic is the culmination of everything he’d been working toward his entire career; the years since still feel like his own response to it. Everybody remembers the diner scene, but the bank heist sequence feels like a magic trick even more today.

#6827. This St. Bart’s House Was Designed to Look Nothing Like Its Neighbors

Built for maximum family time, a converted boutique hotel puts the emphasis on entertaining, hanging out, and blending into the surroundings.

Few clients ask their designer to make the bedrooms in their vacation home smaller and simpler, but that was just the edit the homeowners made to plans for their house in St. Bart’s, says designer David Ries. “They didn’t want these humongous bedrooms with huge floating beds and massive bathrooms.” Instead, the couple wanted to be able to host as many people as possible, a request that didn’t surprise Ries, who’d already worked with the family on homes in Manhattan, Long Island, and Telluride. “It’s what this client has always asked for. They can’t have enough bedrooms.”

Situated on a steep hillside above one of St. Bart’s famously pristine white sand beaches, the house had initially been a boutique hotel. The plan was to turn it into a retreat for themselves and their adult children and grandchildren. The family are longtime visitors to the island—“the daughter told me it was her ‘happy place,’” says Ries, lead designer of the New York-based interior design firm Ries Hayes—and knew exactly what they wanted. Not the white box look popular with luxury new builds on the island, but instead something reminiscent of the “old St. Bart’s,” says East Hampton-based landscape designer Edwina von Gal, who, like Ries, has collaborated with the clients on other projects.

A Caribbean island is a challenging place to work under any circumstances. Everything has to be brought in from far away, including all the plants, which came by barge from Miami. St. Bart’s in particular requires anyone building to work with a local architect and engineer. In their initial proposal, this French team defaulted to that “slick and modernist” look, as von Gal describes it, with the oversized bedrooms and white color scheme that dominates much of the island’s luxury housing stock.

The design team pushed back—asking for a lot of bedrooms (eight) and generous living spaces. They also had strong opinions on the building materials. Given the island’s climate, the humid, salt-soaked air can be hard on everything. “If we did white paint, which is prominent throughout St. Bart's, it’s constant maintenance, constant painting,” says Ries. The design team insisted on cladding the buildings in Ipe, the durable tropical hardwood, which wears well. It mystified the local architects and set the complex apart from its neighbors. It also makes the buildings disappear into the greenery of the hillside.

But the architects quickly caught on to the new plans, and von Gal is eager to praise their technical accomplishments. Regulations require properties to conserve rainwater, so there are cisterns beneath the buildings, plus technology infrastructure and laundry facilities. “Working on the hill is so complex,” she says. “It's such a tight little package of technology and function. What they did was amazing.”

The team decided to reconfigure the property as a series of pavilions that face the view of the ocean and surrounding hills. The main entry is at the top of the steep hill, along with parking. The next level down is for family bedrooms; below that is a primary suite and study; and finally the fourth level is the main living space, including a playroom. “We made sure this playroom was front and center,” says Ries. “My client loves her grandkids.”

The playroom and study have built-in daybeds that can be used as spare guest rooms in a pinch. But the clients did get their eight bedrooms. “We know how to make compact bedrooms and make them very efficient, make them very livable, which allows us to fit in a lot of space for a lot of guests, and then that puts a lot of emphasis on having main entertaining spaces that are big and gracious,” says Ries. “The idea is that everybody hangs out together.”

Everything is united by generous outdoor spaces, which wind between the bedrooms. “Every room has an amazing view,” says Ries. “It feels almost like your own little treehouse in a way.” The bottom living area opens out to a pool and an expansive deck where the family eats most of their meals.

The house has an elevator, but stairs are the primary way the family moves between levels and finally down to the beach. “We hung on for a really great, gracious, fun set of stairs,” says von Gal. “They really introduce you to how amazing the different levels are and all the wonderful secret places.”

Durable fabrics and materials were chosen throughout and Ries made sure everything could be easily moved around and reconfigured. Outside on the main deck, three long dining tables from Sutherland were outfitted with casters so they could be maneuvered into a single long table or several smaller ones; 26 lightweight dining chairs can be pulled from different parts of the deck. If the infamous “Christmas winds” bring a downpour, the tables can even be moved indoors so a dinner party can proceed in inclement weather. Outdoor seating can be arranged as a 15-foot sofa or several love seats.

“It's so flexible,” says Ries. “We know this from working with a client that their needs for entertaining change every day, or sometimes a couple times a day.”

The kitchen is also efficient, designed to produce easy, clean meals. The family would rather spend time in the pool than over a stove. And no part of the living room is oriented around a TV. “They're readers,” says Ries. “There’s always stacks of new books when you go to their homes. So there’s all these cool little spots you can tuck into.”

Ultimately, the client and design team’s insistence on what they wanted paid off—but it wasn’t without a few moments of doubt. “When so many people are telling you ‘no, surely this is not what you want,’ it does start to erode your confidence a little,” says von Gal. “Then when it does come out well, there’s a real sense of triumph and satisfaction.”

Elizabeth Angell is a journalist and editor who writes about style, travel, parenting, and the British Royal Family. She has written for The New York Times and Town & Country and was most recently the Editor in Chief of Romper. She lives in Brooklyn, New York with her family.

#6826. The Best Treadmills for Your Home Gym, Tested by Fitness Editors

We've logged hundreds of miles on each during our testing.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

THE TREADMILL IS one of the most basic and essential tools of a good cardio routine. Being able to move your own bodyweight from Point A to Point B is pretty critical in daily life as well, making walking and running the most functional exercises around. But what if you don’t want to drop money on a monthly membership for a gym 30 minutes away, just to use their banged up and outdated machines?

A great treadmill can be a powerful ally in helping you keep up your fitness and not miss a step in your training. If you’re planning to purchase a treadmill for your home, you should know that it’s no small investment. But with the high level of competition in the marketplace and more stock available than years past, now is a perfect time to take the leap. Whether you want to train for a marathon, build endurance with intense weighted-vest walks on an incline, get the blood pumping between errands, or all of the above, there’s a machine out there for you.

Out of all the treadmills we've tested, we believe that the NordicTrack T10 Treadmill is a reliable choice for most people to use at home, given its durability, performance, and overall value. Its design is simplistic and user-friendly, with a modest touchscreen that offers preset programs and entertainment. For those seeking an added layer of motivation, an optional subscription for on-board workout content is available as an add-on.

We’ve put together a guide to the best treadmills, tested by our editors (who happen to be certified trainers), that will help you torch calories and chase your fitness goals at home.

The NordicTrack 1750 has been a long-standing favorite among MH fitness editors and hardcore indoor runners for years, and with good reason: It’s an all-around winner. The 16-inch touchscreen is responsive and compatible with iFIT programming, and the newest edition features both incline and decline.

The 1750 is one of the most stable treadmills we've ever been on, likely thanks to its durable build and powerful 3.5 CHP motor. The frame doesn't buckle or shake at all when you are putting in those hard miles. Other things to love include the 22-inch by 60-inch deck, which is outfitted with generous cushioning, and the new lower step up height, making it more accessible for all runners. We enjoy how cushioned the landing feels with our feet being cradled with each stride. This treadmill offers speeds of up to 12 MPH and an incline of up to 12%, meaning it can keep up with most running goals. This treadmill is much quieter than other models that we've run on in the past. Whether we're doing incline jogs or finishing with sprints, it can handle it all.NordicTrack's iFit content bring a lot of value, and the addition of this programming to your use of the treadmill can really elevate it. Most of the time, when you are getting technology with next-level technology, you have to be delicate with the machinery. But the 1750 is built like a tank, and in our testing was able to withstand our most punishing sprint sessions.

The only two things we'd change on this machine would be to extend the 10-year frame warranty (we'd love for it to be 15) and make the machine easier to move. Otherwise, this treadmill is a sure-fire winner.

Need an affordable treadmill that can satisfy tight space constraints? The XTERRA TR75 Sport Series is the best machine we've tested that fits the bill, all for just around $1,000. Even less if it happens to be on sale. Despite its low cost, it delivers a level of reliability uncommon among similarly priced treadmills.

During our testing, we were surprised at how quiet the belt and motor performed when maxing out at 12 MPH and 12% incline, though the machine did whine a bit as it adjusted to our desired incline. It is extremely lightweight and compact, yet the steel frame feels solid, and the foldable design adds to its practicality for home use.

The 'Lift Assist and Slow Deploy' mechanism exceeded our initial expectations, making it ridiculously effortless to fold and unfold the machine. It also has wheels. This is a machine that won't cause stress, a ton of logistical planning, and doesn't require multiple hands if you ever need to relocate from one room to another. One person could manage it alone, no problem.

Unlike treadmills with interactive touchscreens, this model is a little less tech-savvy, with a 6.5-inch LCD display. While it does offer some built-in training programs and Bluetooth connectivity for third-party apps that we found intuitive to work with, it lacks the smart features and finishes of higher-end models. But after all, at this price point, it isn't pretending to compete in that class of cardio machines.

Above all, this treadmill boasts a great value. In our experience testing budget-to-premium treadmills, a lower price tag often comes at the expense of build quality, weight capacity, and deck size. The TR75 defies this trend. Its 350-pound user weight capacity surpasses the industry standard, the 60-inch by 20-inch deck offers ample space suitable for taller individuals, and it provides enough power to meet most training needs.

The NordicTrack T10 treadmill takes the lead in the tech-forward, reasonably-priced T series. For those who would like a slightly smaller, less expensive version with all the same high-tech features that the Commercial 1750 comes with, this is the treadmill for you.

When using this machine, we were impressed by the solid build and easy-to-use controls. The touchscreen display is flanked by quick key buttons to adjust speed and incline to your liking in one touch, instead of repeatedly pressing a button while trying to speed up or slow down.

Equipped with a 3.0 continuous HP motor, this treadmill offers the industry standard speed of up to 12 MPH and an incline of up to 12%. Much like other models in the NordicTrack system, this piece of equipment handles impact well. Like the more expensive options from the brand, you feel cushioned and cradled with each stride.

One of the great features of this model is its storability, which comes without sacrificing a whole lot of features. They have even been able to find a way to keep the belt size as 60 inches, the same as the more premium NordicTrack options.

This model also features a 10-inch HD touchscreen display that provides on-demand instructor-led classes and programs to guide your runs at any time. The touchscreen display is smaller than other high-tech models, but it gets the job done.

Part of the new Strive Series from REP Fitness, this curved treadmill takes the category and puts all other designs on notice. Offering a more ergonomic running experience, the curved treadmill burns more calories while reducing impact on your joints.

When we say durable, we mean it. This manual machine can support users up to 400 pounds, and we never had to worry about its tech features glitching or lagging—because there aren't any. Since this machine relies entirely on the user's power (not an external power source), I could maneuver it on its wheels from the garage to the driveway for a breath of fresh air whenever I felt like it.

It's worth noting, however, that moving this treadmill up or down a flight of stairs without multiple pairs of hands isn't advisable. It weighs 418 pounds and lacks any folding or compacting capabilities, so it requires a team to unbox and set up, plus a permanent space to house this beast.

The Peloton Tread takes on the familiar feeling of a live studio experience, initially popularized by the Peloton Bike and compatible app. We recommend the Peloton Tread for those who like a group class environment from the comfort of their own home.

We found that the massive 24" HD touchscreen simulates the camaraderie of running in a race better than any other machine, fostering a competitive-yet-supportive class environment. And now with the new Cross-Training version, the screen swivels fully, allowing you to use it for the strength classes available on demand as well.

This experience helps to increase how much people actually use their treadmills. Translation: Sure, this baby is expensive, but it’s worth the money. It’s an experience unlike any other that pretty much everyone has tried to copy.

The 59-inch by 20-inch deck is large enough for almost any-sized runner, and the 300-pound user weight capacity hits the industry standards we’d expect to see. The newly-added Tread lock makes this machine (now) safe to have around kids and pets who may be tempted to climb and play on it.

Despite the lofty price point, be sure to avoid the temptation of buying a discounted used machine and get yours new. In the past, it's unfortunately been prone to maintenance-related issues, so you’ll want a warranty in case anything happens to yours.

If you're a serious runner looking for a treadmill that can handle serious training, this is it. It's an absolute powerhouse. The frame itself is exceptionally sturdy, and we didn't detect any shaking even when running at a push pace (it goes up to 12 MPH). We also find this treadmill especially great for interval training and hills when we want to switch things up, given its broad decline/incline range.

For bigger users looking to boost their cardio, this treadmill has a 350-pound user weight capacity, surpassing the industry standard by 100 pounds. On top of that, it was built to take a beating, so it can handle any exercise you throw at it, no matter the intensity.

The downside of this machine's durability and solid feel is that it's (unsurprisingly) quite heavy. Weighing in at 323 pounds, moving it requires at least two pairs of hands (and some gusto), even with the included transport wheels. For this reason, you'll need to designate a dedicated spot for this treadmill, as it's not as compact as some other models when folded. That spot will need to be quite spacious, as this treadmill is wider (and a tad longer) than most comparable home machines.

On the plus side, the extra-large 22-inch by 60-inch deck more than compensates for its substantial footprint. We can open up our stride on this machine and never feel crammed or cut short. Bowflex's commitment to quality is evident in its warranty, which offers 15-year frame coverage (on par with the industry standard).

It's also remarkably easy to set up, thanks to straightforward assembly instructions that take less than an hour to complete.

When you are building a home gym, space is a precious resource, and treadmills can take up a significant amount. Be sure to check the dimensions of any treadmill you're considering. Add at least 1-2 feet in every direction, and 3 feet or more behind the treadmill (the area you step onto the machine from) for maximum safety.

One thing buyers also tend to overlook is the height. You’ll want at least 15” inches above your own height when standing on the treadmill. So, if you’re 5’10” (70 inches), make sure your ceiling height is enough to accommodate 85 inches, plus the height of the treadmill belt. This is especially important if you’re planning to use your treadmill for running.

Lastly, consider how much storage space you'll need if you aren’t planning to leave the treadmill out when you aren’t working out. Great folding treadmill options and models can be moved or positioned easily, helping you make the most of the space between runs.

Looking for a treadmill suitable for running? Or maybe you just want a reliable machine to walk on while you watch TV. Whatever you’re looking for, make sure the deck size suits your activity preferences (and your height). The industry standard for a walking treadmill deck is 55 inches by 20 inches, and at least 60 inches by 20 inches for running. You may be able to get away with running or walking on shorter decks, but that means altering your stride length, so you’ll have to think about every step you take.

This might seem obvious, but double-check that all four corners of the treadmill are firmly on the floor after setup. Many home floors aren’t 100% level (you'd be surprised). Make sure your treadmill is well-planted to minimize the risk of slippage, shifting, or tilting.

Treadmills can—and often do—make a lot of noise, especially for your downstairs neighbors (if you live in an apartment) or housemates (if you live in a multi-story house). If you think this might be an issue, consider a treadmill mat to help minimize the noise. These simple, usually affordable mats can help deaden sound and vibration on the floor beneath a treadmill. However, they won't quiet the belt itself, and we’ve suggested models that won’t screech above your gym playlist.

Treadmills, especially large and commercial models, can draw significant power. If your home’s electrical setup allows, dedicate one circuit to just your treadmill. This helps ensure other power-hungry devices on that same circuit don’t blow a fuse.

Nobody wants to spend money on a treadmill that won’t keep up with their workouts. Decide what speeds and inclines you'll need for your training goals. Treadmills vary in capacity for these features, and while 12 mph might be enough for some, it may not be enough for sprinters.

The same goes with inclines. Most brands are transparent about the motor’s horsepower, but some don’t disclose it on the treadmill’s spec sheet. For reference, you should look for at least a 3.0 CHP motor if you plan to run or sprint regularly.

The industry standard for a treadmill frame warranty is 15 years to life. This is the standard for mid- to high-dollar treadmills, but if you’re buying some random machine off of Amazon for $200, don’t expect this type of guarantee. We also recommend registering your machine on the brand’s website right after purchase to make sure you can cash in on the warranty if you ever need to.

As the Fitness and Reviews Editor at Men’s Health, it’s my job to test all kinds of home gym equipment, including treadmills. Our team at MH includes former athletes, marathoners, and certified trainers, and we conferenced on the best treadmills for all types of use. Over the years, our dedicated editors have rigorously tested treadmills from reliable, top-rated brands, evaluating performance, user experience, size, durability, and overall value.

To ensure a comprehensive assessment of each product, the team put each treadmill through the wringer with a variety of workouts, including sprints, long-distance runs, and tempo runs. We did this at home, in the Men's Health gym, and at various training facilities around the world. Together, we've logged hundreds of miles on each treadmill, giving us valuable insights into how it stacks up against other options on the market.

We also made sure we're up to date on industry standards (motor horsepower, warranty, incline/decline options, and speed) and the components that make an excellent treadmill versus a ‘meh’ one. From that research and testing, we deduced the front runners and best treadmills in the pack.

Maybe. It all depends on your personal fitness level and goals. Curved treadmills are non-motorized. They rely instead on you walking or running, and the belt being pushed backward manually with every foot strike on the sloped surface. While this might not seem as "fancy" or high-tech as a powered treadmill, this design has its benefits.

"Curved treadmills tend to recruit more muscles due to the shape of the belt and the act of propelling against it," says Eric Sung, C.S.C.S., a member of the Men's Health Strength in Diversity initiative and trainer at Performance Lab in New York City. "It also eases the strain of your joints because of the curvature and is often better for users with varied joint pain."

By some reports, exercising on a curved treadmill burns 30% more calories over a traditional powered treadmill.

"One drawback of a curved treadmill is that it is difficult to achieve higher speeds (sprints) with proper technique. This can impact front side mechanics with one's stride through by 'reaching'," explains Sung. Another thing to consider: Because curved treads are motorless, they also don't allow for incline or decline capabilities.

Yes, although not critically, according to David Siik, founder of Precision Run by Equinox.

"To create a sense of security, people tend to run far too close to the front of the treadmill," he explains. This can lead to a compressed posture, restricting the range of motion in your arms. Siik recommends maintaining at least one foot of distance between your belly and the treadmill monitor.

Treadmills may require slightly less engagement of our hamstrings compared to outdoor running, though the difference isn't substantial, Siik tells us.

"Another important point we emphasize with our runners and walkers is the importance of avoiding the habit of constantly staring at your phone while using the treadmill." Over time, this posture can strain your neck.

Jonathan Cane, an exercise physiologist, running coach, and founder of City Coach Multisport in New York City, highlights several benefits of running on a treadmill. He mentions that treadmills allow for the simulation of terrain, making them valuable for training when natural hills or elevation changes aren't readily available. This feature is particularly useful for preparing for hilly races.

"Walking up a grade can have comparable cardiovascular benefit to running flat, yet doesn't have the associated orthopedic stress, so it can be a great way to get a workout without all the pounding you might have with running," he explains.

Siik explains that the benefits of running and walking on an incline boil down to the increased energy spend (remaining calories burned) caused by the increased power needed to climb vertically.

"Variation is key to experience the full benefits of incline. Steep inclines have wonderful energy burning benefits but also can destabilize the knees over time if done frequently," he explains. "This is why the treadmill is beautiful for incline training; it allows you to play with such a spectrum of programming."

Treadmills also offer the advantage of being weather-independent, with modern models featuring softer surfaces that are gentler on the body compared to running on hard asphalt or concrete. (Remember: Running outside exposes you to the elements, which can have additional mental and physical benefits, so do it when possible.)

#6819. The Hybrid Training Formula to Run Fast and Lift Heavy

Get the split right, then pick shoes that match the work.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

RUNNING SOMETIMES GETS a bad rap in the weight room, as if a few laps around the block will somehow undo all those hard-earned gains. Turns out, the two activities aren’t the rivals they’re made out to be. Adding miles to your strength routine can build endurance to back up all that muscle. A review study found that combining aerobic exercise with resistance training produced gains in muscle size and maximal strength similar to lifting alone. The benefits work in reverse, too: Smart strength work can also help make you a more efficient runner.

The result? A body that can move serious weight, cover some ground, and handle whatever else you throw at it. Of course, getting the most out of running and lifting takes some strategy, from how you schedule your workouts to how you manage recovery. Another important factor is foot health, which can affect both your big lifts and running splits.

Here’s how to balance running and lifting in your routine, strengthen the feet that support both, and choose shoes for each workout.

YOUR TRAINING SCHEDULE should reflect your priorities. Focused on building strength? Keep your toughest gym sessions front and center and work easy-paced runs around them. Training for a race? Schedule your key runs first. “If your goal is strength, you can’t show up tired,” says Courtney Conley, DC, a chiropractic physician specializing in foot mechanics who recommends separating demanding running and lifting workouts when possible to allow for recovery. Either way, she suggests hitting the weights two to three times a week without going all-out every session.

But if you’re running and strength training, consider also building your explosive power. “You have to train the spring, train that reactive quickness,” Conley says. This is where plyometrics like jumps and hops train you to generate force quickly, absorb impact, and rebound. And, this comes in handy whether you’re sprinting, changing direction on the court, or charging uphill. It will also help your big lifts in the gym, particularly squats, deadlifts, and any type of Olympic lift because it develops things like neuromuscular coordination—allowing you to implement full-body movement patterns and stabilize heavy loads faster; and the rate of force development—how fast you can turn your strength on.

Just make sure you train this way early in your workout, not at the end. “You don’t want to do plyometric training when you’re fatigued,” Conley cautions. Doing so can compromise your form, making those explosive moves less effective and potentially increasing your risk of injury.

THE BODYPART THAT makes all of this training possible is your feet. Their small muscles help you maintain balance, support your arches, and control movement under load. During a squat, for example, those muscles help provide a stable base as you drive through your legs and hips. “Deadlifting is a foot action,” Conley explains. “You’ve got to feel your mid foot and press it into the ground to stabilize your body.”

Running demands just as much. Every stride is essentially a single-leg hop, with your feet and ankles absorbing impact, storing energy, and releasing it to propel you forward. In her practice, Conley sometimes sees clients with weak feet or Achilles issues. “When they go to hop, they sink into the ground, and there’s just no spring out of that system.” It’s one more reason those muscles below your ankles deserve just as much attention as your quads and glutes.

And while you could do specific mobility exercises for your feet, those small muscles get quite a workout when performing single-leg moves that help build strength, balance, and stability.

WHEN YOU TRADE weights for miles, what you need underfoot changes. Your plush running shoes may be great on the road, but they’re no match for the weight room. “When you’re in the gym and you want to lift heavy things, you cannot be wearing your 45 millimeter stack height cushion shoe that has a toe spring on it,” Conley says. “You can’t feel the ground.” For strength training, look for a firm, relatively flat sole that keeps you grounded when the weights get heavy.

One thing to consider is looking for a shoe that allows for balanced weight distribution across the foot. Another is a wide toe-box that allows for your toes to splay. When your big toe has the space to remain straight, it can help keep you stable. It’s this last principle behind the Altra Fit that allows your foot to function naturally: a roomy toe box that gives your toes space while keeping the midfoot secure, rather than making the whole shoe wider. “The widest part of your foot should be your toes,” Conley explains.

And it is this design that can help you choose shoes for running, strength training, or both, that allow your feet to get stronger during all these activities because they support them. “The whole reason I love Altra running shoes so much is because they respect the anatomy of the foot,” Conley says.

Altra offers different cushioning levels, stack heights, and heel-to-toe drops. “If you’re new to this conversation of foot health and strength training, you cannot go zero to 100,” Conley says. In other words, match your shoes to the workout, and if you’re switching to lower-stack or zero-drop footwear, give your feet and calves time to adapt. Here are some Altra shoes to consider:

If you are someone who goes from the weight room to a run (or vice versa), the Escalante 5 keeps things lean with a 24mm stack that has a zero-drop and wide toebox that gives you a stable platform during your heaviest lifts. And for your runs, the responsive EGO cushioning allows you to strengthen your foot with each step no matter whether you are leisurely jogging, crushing speedwork, or doing errands.

If you like to hit the trails with some weight training on the side, the Lone Peak 9 adds a little more cushioning and a grippy MaxTrac outsole for tackling rocky, uneven trails. It also features the Altra’s Original FootShape™ fit and zero-drop platforms that keep your heel and forefoot level.

#6811. Fascia Release Is the Key to Regulating Your Nervous System, Getting Better Sleep, and Improving Skin

Fascia, the thin web that envelops the face and body, is more powerful than you might think. Here’s how to release it

I’m curled up on a treatment bed in the Corinthia spa, feeling a knot of tension dissipate slowly from my neck and shoulders. Within minutes, tears prick my eyes. I’m familiar with the sudden catharsis bodywork can bring, but this is different. Known as “myofascial release”—a targeted therapy that works with fascia, the continuous matrix of connective tissue that wraps every muscle, nerve, and organ—it aims to reduce tightness and release restricted tissue, restoring its natural glide.

“Fascia is deeply connected to the entire sensory network,” explains Nadia Alibhai, a London-based osteopath who was unfazed by my heightened emotions. “When you move or hold stress, forces are transmitted through this interconnected system. It senses everything and dictates how the whole body responds. This treatment directly influences how we perceive and react to tension.”

Once dismissed as mere biological wrapping paper, fascia—made up of water, collagen, elastin, and hyaluronic acid—is now understood as a living, three-dimensional web. “It’s part of a complex system involving movement, circulation, lymphatics, posture, and tissue health,” Alibhai explains. When it works well, it keeps everything moving and prevents systems from becoming sluggish, but when it becomes restricted, often from scrolling, sedentary habits, or chronic stress, the fluid between the layers can thicken. This affects physical mobility and emotional well-being, leading to issues ranging from pain and stiffness to a dysregulated nervous system.

Fascia and neurology are intrinsically linked, explains skin-health expert Annee de Mamiel. “Fascia changes according to how we move, breathe, and repeatedly load the body,” she says. “Cortisol and adrenaline change our physiology; the nervous system alters breathing, muscular tone, and stress response. Over time, these repeated patterns affect the tissues.” It’s a catch-22: greater stress can tighten fascia, which in turn can send your body into fight-or-flight mode, prolonging the anxiety that caused the damage in the first place.

This mind-body connection is just one reason why fascia has piqued the wellness industry’s interest. Google searches for “fascia release” have soared in the past year, while facials targeting the system are becoming more popular. Robert Schleip, the director of the Fascia Research Group at Ulm University, attributes this to its holistic possibilities. “Fascia is now studied in relation to the autonomic nervous system, and therefore emotional regulation, as well as the immune system and aging,” he says. The latest findings show that supporting your fascia goes far beyond easing a tight neck; it's linked to reduced arterial stiffness and improved cardiovascular health.

Fascia release has also entered the skin-health conversation. Tight fascia can be an overlooked culprit behind loss of luminosity, puffiness, and premature aging, says de Mamiel, because it’s heavily involved in fluid drainage and lymphatic pathways. “The diaphragm plays a role in pressure changes and fluid movement through the body, and the fascial system connects these areas,” she explains. “When I work on movement and tension through the neck, chest, and rib cage, I often see a change in how congested or puffy the face appears.” However, releasing fascia effectively doesn't require aggressive force. “If I use so much pressure that your body braces, I’ve produced the opposite response—it’s about creating conditions where the nervous system and tissue can both settle.”

Facialist Nichola Joss achieves similarly impressive results with her signature Inner Facial, which fuses lymphatic drainage, fascia release, buccal massage, and sculpting techniques. “Fascia surrounds and supports our muscles; when restricted, it affects how freely muscles move,” Joss explains. This affects lymphatic flow and your skin’s natural glow. “By releasing muscular and fascial tension, you lift, tone, and sculpt contours while boosting circulation and softening fine lines.”

Beyond professional treatments, supporting fascial health depends on consistent daily habits. First, fascia thrives on varied movement: try walking, twisting your body, reaching overhead, and squatting to support overall health. Unfortunately, there are no true quick fixes: Spending the day glued to an office chair and then going straight into an intense HIIT session can actually be counterproductive. Instead, interrupt patterns throughout the day: Stand during phone calls instead of sitting, swing your arms gently during screen breaks, and make sure you’re not holding your breath during stressful periods at work. Counteract hours spent hunched over a device with this simple doorway stretch: Place your forearms against the frame, step forward gently, and hold for 20 to 30 seconds while breathing deeply.

Schleip recommends aligning exercise with your circadian rhythm. “Incorporate dynamic, elastic, or bouncing movements in the morning, ideally outdoors, and slow stretches in the evening,” he suggests, adding that sustained yoga positions, such as a child’s pose held for at least 30 seconds, can be particularly helpful. “Regular stretching improves joint mobility and may benefit cardiovascular markers such as arterial stiffness,” he adds. Finally, make nutrition and hydration a priority: fascia is 70 percent water and needs hydration to glide freely.

As Alibhai reflects at the end of my session: “The goal isn’t to constantly release fascia; it’s to keep your body moving, adaptable, and well nourished.” I leave feeling lighter and more grounded—and I instinctively skip the Tube and walk home.

#6805. A Landmark All-Lalanne Sale Is Heading to Sotheby’s

Expected to fetch as much as $25 million, “Les Lalanne: Beyond Imagination” is the first-ever various-owner auction dedicated to Claude and François-Xavier Lalanne.

The market for Claude and François-Xavier Lalanne’s irreverent creations has been on a high lately. In April, a single wooly sheep sculpture by François-Xavier fetched $2.3 million. Over the summer, a quartet of his frog fountains smashed their $3.5 million estimate to achieve nearly $6.3 million. And the record for most expensive collectible design work belongs to a set of flower-adorned bronze mirrors crafted by Claude: They netted $33.5 million earlier this year, eclipsing François-Xavier’s $31.4 million hippo bar.

Now, ELLE Decor can exclusively share that Sotheby’s will host the first-ever auction dedicated to Les Lalanne, featuring pieces sourced from various collections. On December 8, more than 30 works by the late French artist duo will hit the block in “Les Lalanne: Beyond Imagination.” It is expected to fetch in the region of $25 million.

“Les Lalanne have become a global phenomenon, changing the way generations of collectors think about design,” says Jodi Pollack, chairman of 20th century design and major collections for the Americas. “A sheep can be a seat, a crocodile can be a console, and both can hold their own alongside the most important works in a collection.”

The collection spans everything from a gold-patinated bronze gorilla (estimated at $8–12 million) to a white marble owl (estimated at $4–6 million), and a flock of five sheep, including two lambs ($300,000–500,000 each). Highlights also include lesser-known motifs, such as a playful pair of bronze monkeys with deep-green patination that makes them almost look like jade ($800,000–1.2 million each), and two matching crocodile consoles ($700,000–1 million) that complement the famous chairs Claude created after obtaining a deceased crocodile from the Paris Zoo.

Those hoping to get an in-person glimpse at the creatures can see the menagerie starting December 2 at the Breuer building, Sotheby’s new Manhattan headquarters. To accompany the show, the auction house enlisted Milan-based artist Gaia Alari to devise custom illustrations for the catalogue, which reads like a fairy tale.

“The story begins in the catalogue and comes to life at the Breuer, where visitors will step into an enchanted winter garden and meet the cast,” says Pollack. “It’s our way of celebrating these works and the spirit in which they were conceived. We want visitors to look at familiar things as Claude and François-Xavier did and to see the extraordinary in the ordinary.”

“Les Lalanne: Beyond Imagination” will be on view at Sotheby’s New York from December 2 to 7. The live auction will take place on December 8, 2026, at Sotheby’s New York at 11 a.m..

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6797. The Oura Ring 5 Is on Sale for the First Time This Black Friday

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

THE OURA RING has been my go-to daily fitness tracker for almost a decade, and for Black Friday, Oura is offering a massive discount on their latest model. Right now, every single style of the Oura Ring 5 is $100 off. That’s anywhere from 20 to 25 percent off something that you can use daily to track your health metrics, from sleep to activity.

If you’ve been interested in getting the Oura Ring 5, now’s your time. The Oura Ring 5 was just released this summer, which is why we haven’t seen any offers like this yet. And after this event ends, it’s unlikely it will go on sale again soon. So if you are looking for its cutting-edge smart ring tech at a discount, this is your best opportunity. Also, heads up: if you have an FSA or HSA plan, Oura Ring 5 is covered.

Here is everything you need to know about the Oura Ring Black Friday and Cyber Monday sale.

The Oura Ring 5 is Oura's latest offering, and a few upgrades earned it a spot on our Best Smart Rings list. This version has no noticeable sensors, while previous models had three interior nodes that could be a bit uncomfortable. It still collects health data seamlessly.

The Oura Ring 5 is lighter and slimmer than any previous models. The ring provides real-time stress tracking, continuous heart-rate monitoring, and more precise temperature sensing. All of this is designed to give you more precise, helpful feedback throughout the day. The ring’s interior has also changed from an epoxy to all-over titanium for a lighter feel.

The charging case is another great upgrade available to Oura Ring 5 users. This case lets you store a charge so you can power up your ring without being limited by nearby outlets. This means you can keep an extra charge for your ring while you're traveling or on the move so you don't miss out on any insights. The new charging dock is also improved and can charge your ring in about an hour.

Oura recommends wearing the ring on your forefinger, since that's where your pulse is strongest, which can mean slightly better data, but really any finger works. I know a few people who use their Oura Ring as their wedding band. So if you know your ring finger size you’re good to go on that.

If you're unsure about your size, it could be a good idea to order one of Oura's sizing kits. It’s now $10 on Amazon, and that gets applied to your future ring purchase, so technically it's "free." And it allows you to lock in the sale.

When you purchase on ouraring.com the process of getting a sizing kit is a little more involved. You order the ring first, and then they send you a sizing kit to use to get your correct size in the system. Depending on which route you choose for the sizing kit, you'll use that same route to purchase your ring when you decide to buy.

If you want to make sure you lock in the sale, you can purchase the size you feel is right, and as long as you return it within 30 days, you can resize after. You can find all of the details on those returns and exchanges on their site.

You might not be looking for a super-slim smart ring. If you’re used to wearing rings with some weight, or you just like something with a little more heft, the Oura Ring 4 is the first version I think is worth keeping, even if you’re buying a new Oura Ring 5.

Look at you thinking ahead, you! Great question. To keep things short and sweet: It’s almost too late, but you can still beat the clock with a few methods. #6793. The Beverly Hills Hotel Is Getting Its Biggest Update Since the 1940’s

Old Hollywood glamour meets contemporary panache in the hotel’s landmark refresh.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Above: The script logo was designed by Paul R. Williams in the 1940s.

“I love the romance of the past,” says Ken Fulk, mastermind of the most significant updates to the Beverly Hills Hotel since the ’40s, when Paul R. Williams created many of its beloved spaces and its pink and green palette. Fulk has refreshed terraces and devised what he calls “the prettiest dining room in all of L.A., a very 1920s library bar, and an over-the-top Copacabana-style supper club,” opening early next year.

An unabashed Hollywood fanboy, Fulk aimed for old-school glamour with contemporary panache. “In my mind,” he says, “Marlene Dietrich and Cary Grant could walk through the lot.”
